D. SCHOOLS AND SCHOOL DISTRICTS 1. THE DEVELOPMENT AND PURPOSE OF SCHOOLS Schools Developed Early in Our National Life. Schools 

(and education) have always been respected in the United States; they have become an important factor in our civil life. The early schools of the colonists were closely connected with the churches, and were often taught by clergymen. But as the early communities grew in population and wealth, tax supported (or public) schools gradually came into existence. As early as 1642, the MASSACHUSETTS COL- 

ONY enacted a law requiring all communities of a certain size to maintain a public school. Gradually this same interest in popular education spread throughout most of the colonies, especially those of NEW ENGLAND. When the new

Federal government in 1787 passed the Northwest Ordinance, governing the newly acquired Northwest Territory, it contained a clause which stated that "schools and the means of education shall forever be encouraged." This idea not only permeated the Northwest Territory as it developed into states, but also spread throughout the entire nation. 

These colonial and pioneer schools were simple indeed when compared with our modern schools. The teachers had little or no training for their work, and the school houses were poorly equipped with such necessary materials as books and maps.¹ Girls were seldom permitted to attend as much as boys, until such noble leaders as MARY LYON and EMMA WILLARD succeeded in getting a movement started which gave girls some educational rights and opportunities.2 'Read the description of Ichabod Crane's school and schoolhouse as told in Washington Irving's famous story The Legend of Sleepy 

Hollow.

It describes in a humorous and exaggerated way a pioneer 

New England School. 

'Look up the lives of and report to your class on the work of 

Mary Lyon, Emma Willard, and Horace Mann. Show what each contributed to the progress of American education. 135

(See also page 288.)

Why We Maintain Schools. As the nation developed, it early realized that a successful democracy needs an educated citizenry. In order to vote wisely, voters must know something about the world in which they live. They must be able to read; they must be able to form intelligent opinions about political issues. They must have a sympathetic attitude toward their fellowmen. 

In general, seven great ideals have been set up, which the public schools of our country try to instill into the minds of boys and girls so that they will grow up to be desirable citizens of the United States. These IDEALS are commonly called the Seven Cardinal Principles of American 

Education. They are: 

1. Good health and safety. 

2. Worthy home membership. 

3. Mastery of the tools and spirit of learning. 

4. Desirable citizenship and world goodwill. 

5. Wholesome life work and economic independence. 

6. Wise use of leisure time. 

7. Good character.

These SEVEN GREAT PRINCIPLES OF EDUCATION have been explained to the parents of American boys and girls as follows:¹ 1. Your Child's Health The school provides a SAFE PHYSICAL and MENTAL ENVIRON- 

MENT for your child. The modern school is housed in a clean, comfortable building with proper light, heat, and ventilation; seats that encourage correct posture; sanitary toilets and washrooms. Physical inspection discovers defects and helps to overcome them through medical treatment and corrective exercise. 

Playfields and gymnasiums encourage healthful recreation. The school teaches correct health habits. It protects against contagious diseases. It provides special care for the undernourished, special classes for handicapped children and encourages healthful diet through well-balanced meals. The school creates apprecia- 'Message to parents from the National Committee on American 

Education Week-1932.

tion for health as a foundation of happiness and a vital common enterprise of the race. Health is the first wealth. 

2. Education for Home 

The HOME is the fundamental institution among all civilized peoples. The school is educating your child for worthy membership in the home which you provide for him today and in his own future home. The school maintains the atmosphere of cooperation and unselfishness, which is essential to happiness in home relationships. It teaches your child simple home skills -how to cook, to sew, to repair home equipment, to decorate and furnish tastefully and economically, to manage the family budget, to care for infants, and to nurse the sick. It teaches the principles of sanitation, of home and community planning. The school gives your child training in reading, music, conversation, and recreations which enrich home life. It magnifies the home as one of the finest things in life. 

3. The Tools of Learning 

The most important tools are the TOOLS OF THE MIND-our systems of letters, numbers, symbols, and maps; our mother tongue; our dictionaries, encyclopedias, and handbooks; our world of print and libraries. The school is giving your child possession of these tools. He learns to read rapidly with understanding. He learns the importance of good food for the mind. 

Through association with schoolmates and teachers, he acquires the ability to learn from others. Through class discussion he learns to test and modify his conclusions. He learns to think. 

He becomes familiar with the world of scholarship and science. 

He learns the laws of life and of nature. He studies the great human institutions and ideals. He acquires sound habits of study. He catches the spirit of learning and the good life. To learn is to grow. 

4. Your Child's Citizenship 

The school is preparing your child for the responsibilities and privileges of CITIZENSHIP. By taking part in the simple relation ships of the school, he comes to understand the spirit of fairness, justice, intelligence, and goodwill. He learns the importance of honesty and cooperation. He learns to subordinate his selfish interest to the needs and wishes of others. He learns the history of his country. The nation's founders become his heroes. He studies the principles upon which the nation is builded. His attention is called to important economic and social problems. He learns to gather and weigh facts. He learns to respect the

property and rights of others. He develops the spirit of good sportsmanship; he learns to take responsibility, and to obey established rules. He learns to be loyal to common ideals and purposes. 

5. Your Child's Vocation 

The school is preparing your child to succeed in a CHOSEN VOCATION. It teaches him fundamentals needed in every occupation-reading, writing, and mathematics. It gives him facts relating to various vocations, in order that he may be master rather than slave of the work he chooses. Courses in guidance help your child to find the trade or profession in which he is most likely to succeed because of his interest and ability. Vocational training helps him to develop important skills. A school placement service will help him find a job. In school, your child is establishing habits of industry and thrift which will contribute to his material prosperity. Because the school helps him to do skillfully what he likes best to do through life, he will experience those satisfactions which are the joy of work. 

6. Your Child's Leisure 

Each generation enjoys more LEISURE, as men learn how to make machines work for them on the farms, in the mines, factories, and offices. Rapid transportation, rapid communication, speedier services of all kinds are increasingly saving time. Your child will have more hours free from labor than you have had. 

The school seeks to teach him to use his leisure with discrimination and wisdom. In school, your child learns to appreciate fine companionship, wholesome conversation, the world's finest literature, sculpture, music, and other art. He may be developing skill in one of these arts himself. He is encouraged to pursue worthwhile interests; to learn games for healthful recreation; to swim; to enjoy outdoor life; to prefer those leisure activities which build instead of destroy; to make daily play a source of joy and strength. 

7. Your Child's Character 

Good CHARACTER is the supreme objective of education and of life. In school, worthy standards of conduct are emphasized, and the virtues that underlie excellence and happiness are practised. The school is building character in your child by helping him to achieve physical, mental, and spiritual fitness; by training him to use facts correctly and to weigh evidence carefully; by encouraging him to observe the principles of good behavior as a matter of intelligent action rather than because he fears

A TOWNSHIP CONSOLIDATED SCHOOL 

punishment. The school teaches the lives of men renowned for their nobility of character. It offers opportunity to develop the qualities of honesty, generosity, dependability, and courage which are the glory of good men. The school fosters faith. It commends to youth a belief in God and religion. 

A TYPICAL RURAL SCHOOL

Are you, through your school life, with the help of your teachers, trying to develop these seven great principles in your own life? Without your own aid and effort, the school alone cannot do it. When you consider the large amount of public money that your community spends on the schools, money which your parents have paid in taxes; and when you consider the effect that your schooling can have on your future life, do you not owe it to yourself, to your parents, and to your community to make the best possible use of your school days? 

A PAROCHIAL SCHOOL NEAR THE CHURCH WHOSE PARISH MAINTAINS IT Not All Our Schools Are Public Schools. Many schools are not public (tax supported). Churches, as you know, sometimes maintain schools that are commonly called parochial schools. They are supported by the parish or by some larger unit of the church organization. In addition to the regular school subjects, PAROCHIAL SCHOOLS teach the religious beliefs of the church.

Our government expects that the course of study in PAROCHIAL SCHOOLS will con form to state regulations, and that instruction in the regular school subjects will be kept up to the level of that of

the public schools. Some churches not only maintain common or elementary schools, but high schools and colleges as well.

There are also many private schools throughout the nation, especially in the larger cities. They are usually established and maintained by some interested group of people or by some wealthy individual. PRIVATE SCHOOLS are not supported by taxation, but by private funds. Pupils who enroll in such schools must pay a tuition fee for the privilege of attending. Our government maintains the same 11 131 11 20 דיוננו. SO 

A PRIVATE SCHOOL BUILDING. Coe College, Cedar Rapids, Iowa. 

attitude toward private schools as toward parochial schools. 

Like the parochial schools, private schools are represented in all grades-elementary, high school, and college. 

Occasionally there are people, who for some reason, do not want to send their children either to a public, parochial, or private school; but instead, they engage one or more teachers to come into their homes to instruct their children. 

Such teachers are commonly called tutors. This method of educating children, however, is usually restricted to wealthy people only.

All schools, whether they be public, private, or parochial, have great determining influence on the lives of boys and girls, and consequently, on the men and women of the Nation. Not all American churches today support schools, and comparatively few parents can afford to send their children to private schools or to engage tutors. Therefore, the vast majority of American boys and girls in most communities must get their school education in the tax supported, public schools. For that reason, in our study of civics, we shall be primarily interested in the PUBLIC SCHOOLS, and how they are created and supported. 

2. KINDS OF PUBLIC SCHOOLS Our Educational Ladders. Every healthy, ambitious 

American boy and girl has before him or her, several possible educational ladders to climb. (See page 149.) The first eight grades are common to all, and the junior and senior high schools are becoming more and more common. 

It is estimated that there are now about 5,600,000 boys and girls in American public and private high schools each year. Most small communities still maintain the elementary grade school of eight grades (in some states, seven grades) and a high school of four years. Many of the larger communities, however, are erecting and maintaining a separate school for the seventh, eighth, and ninth grades known as the junior high school.

Since compulsory school attendance laws have become common throughout the country (see page 152), many cities maintain junior and senior technical high schools and vocational schools. Such schools are established for boys and girls who find it impossible, or unprofitable, to attend the regular JUNIOR or SENIOR HIGH SCHOOL, and for those who, for some reason, do not care to attend the regular schools. These TECHNICAL, and VOCATIONAL SCHOOLS usually offer much work along practical lines, such as typewriting, bookkeeping, woodwork and carpentry, and metal work of all kinds. The aim of the work is to teach the young per-

For those pupils who cannot attend full-time, VOCATIONAL SCHOOLS usually have arrangements for part-time attendance. Oftentimes people who have already had some high school work enroll in these schools and frequently high school graduates attend, to learn some special trade.

Public night schools, which are operated during the early evening hours, are becoming common throughout the country. Many cities now maintain such EVENING SCHOOLS for elementary, high, and vocational school work. Social centers, oftentimes kept open evenings, perform useful educational services by offering instruction in practical and in teresting subjects.

3. SCHOOL LAWS AND SCHOOL DISTRICTS Federal Interest in Education. Our Federal government is greatly interested in EDUCATION and tries to promote and encourage it; but the government leaves to the various states the actual control and management of education with the exception of the Army and the Navy schools and of some Indian schools. Since 1862, the Federal government has given to the various states about eleven million acres of land. The proceeds from the sale of this land has estab lished and helped to maintain COLLEGES OF AGRICULTURE, SCIENCE, and THE MECHANICAL ARTS. The states, however, must help support these Land Grant Colleges, as they are called.

In 1917, Congress passed the Smith-Hughes Act, which provides Federal funds for vocational education in the states, if the states furnish an equal amount of money. 

The Act required that this vocational education must be in the field of agriculture, the industrial trades, and home economics.

The Federal government maintains schools for Indians on some Indian reservations, and helps educate others by paying their tuition to regular state schools. Congress also maintains the U. S. Naval Academy at Annapolis, Maryland, and the U. S. Military Academy at West Point, New York.

In addition to these educational projects, the Federal government maintains in the Department of the Interior, a U. S. Office of Education. This OFFICE is headed by a U. S. Commissioner of Education. The OFFICE OF EDUCA- 

TION collects, publishes, and distributes statistics and literature; directs educational surveys; and gives out advice on educational matters. 

Schools Largely Controlled by State Laws. As was pointed out in the preceding paragraphs, the Federal government is interested in education, but it leaves the actual administration of public schools to the individual states. 

Hence, each state has a school system and school laws of

War Department U. S. MILITARY ACADEMY, WEST POINT, N. Y. (See page 220.) 

Navy Department UNITED STATES NAVAL ACADEMY, ANNAPOLIS, MARYLAND

its own. No two states have school systems and laws exactly alike; but all of them are similar. At the head of each state system is a superintendent known by such titles as, state superintendent of schools, commissioner of education, and superintendent of public instruction. In most of the states he is an elected officer. He and his staff of assistants have general supervisory charge of the schools in the state. It is their duty to see that the school laws passed by the state legislature are enforced in the local school districts. 

Compulsory School Attendance Laws. All states now have some kind of a compulsory school attendance law which makes it mandatory for all children between certain fixed ages and who are mentally and physically healthy to attend school for a specified number of days each year. The provisions of these laws in regard to ages, health, number of days of attendance, and exemptions, vary in the different states. You should secure a copy of the COMPULSORY SCHOOL 

ATTENDANCE LAW of your state and be able to answer and discuss intelligently the following questions regarding it:¹ 

1. Between what ages must I attend school? 

2. Must I complete the eighth grade? 

3. Must I complete high school? 

4. May I attend a vocational or other special school, after I have reached a certain age? 

5. What children are exempt from complying with the law? 

6. Who enforces the law? 

7. When and why was the law passed? 

8. What is the penalty if I break the law? Who is punished, my parents or I? 

9. Has the law been made stricter in recent years? 

If so, why? 

¹You may secure a copy by writing to your city, county or state superintendent of schools.

10. Does my state have a child labor law (a law which prohibits me from working for wages outside my home) which helps enforce the school attendance law? If so what are its main provisions?

Who Pays for the Public Schools? Laws concerning schools are usually made by the state legislature, but the money necessary for the establishment and maintenance of schools is raised largely by taxing the people of the local

The states maintain state universities, state teachers' A school district is a quasi-municipality. It is a governmental or political unit or division which exists for a special purpose-to establish and maintain a school. It is not a municipality in a strict sense of the word, but only a partial one, hence the name quasi-municipality is given to it. Its area is often the same as the municipality, be it town, village, or city. However, towns in rural regions are often divided into several school districts. High school districts in rural regions sometimes cover several municipalities. (Review the discussion on political divisions beginning on page 34.)

colleges and other special schools. But, most of the money used for building and maintaining common schools and high schools is derived by taxing the people in the communities (districts) where they are located. Rural Schools in Districts or Towns. In rural regions in many parts of the country, SMALL DISTRICTS maintain a school for their children. Usually these schools are one- 

¹United States Bureau of Education Bulletin No. 2, 1935, p. 46. 

2 Financial Statistics, Bureau of Census, 1931, p. 25.

room buildings with one teacher in charge, who teaches all eight grades. Usually, too, these schools are some distance from a village or city, so that the teacher stays (boards) with a nearby farm family. These schools are managed by a local school board (usually three members) elected by the voters of the district. 

It is difficult, and in most cases impossible, for such a school to offer to the rural boys and girls the same educational advantages that the larger and (financially) wealthier city schools can offer. Accordingly, a movement is 

Louisville and Nashville Railway A "CONSOLIDATED" SCHOOL IN ALABAMA 

Children from a large area are transported by buses to this modern graded school. What advantages do children in such schools have over those in one-room schools? growing throughout the country to unite (consolidate) several rural districts into one. When this is done, the small schools are abandoned, and ONE LARGE GRADED SCHOOL (and sometimes a HIGH SCHOOL) is erected, usually near the center of the newly-created district. Children who live far from the new school are transported to and from it by a system of buses. Such a consolidated school can offer more educational advantages to the boys and girls than a little

one-room school. (Can you make a list of the advantages of such a school? Do you think the cost per pupil is greater or less? Discuss.)

Sometimes such consolidated districts take in an entire 

TOWNSHIP of area and are called township schools. These schools, like small district schools, are usually controlled by an elected board.

Village Schools. A village school district usually has the same boundary as the village; however, in consolidated 

ROOSEVELT SCHOOL, WEST ALLIS, WISCONSIN 

This is typical of elementary schools in villages and small cities. regions the district is extended to take in surrounding rural areas. A village school is managed in much the same way as a rural school. There is a school board, usually elected by the voters of the village. Many villages maintain a high school, as well as a graded school. 

The County Superintendent of Schools. In most states where there are counties (except in New England) there is a county superintendent of schools who has general super-

vision of all the common and high schools in the county except those of the independent cities. He and his assistants (usually called supervisors) help enforce the state school laws; help carry out the suggestions of the state superintendent of schools; and give advice and assistance to the principals, teachers, and school boards under their charge. 

The COUNTY SUPERINTENDENT is either elected by the voters of the county, or is appointed by a county school board. 

City Schools. City school systems are usually independent of any county system, and are subject only to state laws and the state superintendent. City school boards (boards of education) are either elected by the voters at large, or are appointed by the mayor or by some city department. 

The CITY SCHOOL BOARD is sometimes independent in its authority, and sometimes it is subordinate (under control of) to the city council. City school systems are large and must usually maintain many schools. This requires the raising and spending of thousands, and in large cities, millions of dollars yearly. To do this in the best interests of pupils and patrons, the SCHOOL BOARD needs expert help.

In large cities, the BOARD usually employs a business manager or secretary who takes care of the financial and business end of the system. The CITY BOARD always employs a superintendent who takes care of the actual administration of the schools. He, in turn, is usually allowed to appoint his assistants, supervisors, principals, and teachers. 

The entire system and all its employees, of course, are al ways subject to the control of the BOARD OF EDUCATION.

UNIT II. COUNTY GOVERNMENT THE PROBLEM 

In UNIT I of this PART we have considered the machinery of MUNICIPAL GOVERNMENTS-the town, village, city, and school district. If you have studied thoroughly the government of the municipality in which you live, and that of the school district in which you live, you are ready for a study of the next political division in our governmental makeup-the COUNTY. 

It is the purpose of this UNIT to examine the machinery of COUNTY GOVERNMENT, and to show how it plays its part in our entire scheme of democracy.

It will help you answer the question: How is my COUNTY governed?

The Court House is the seat (capitol) of the county government.

1. THE ORIGIN OF AND NEED FOR COUNTY GOVERNMENT The Origin of Counties. The term county is of English origin, the divisions of England being so called. When aristocratic Englishmen developed the plantation system in the early Southern colonies, because of the plantations, it became necessary to have a LOCAL GOVERNMENT which covered a large area. The rich land-owners who controlled the South were few in number. The negro slaves and the poor whites made up the mass of the population, but the latter had little voice in the government. This was quite different from the conditions in the New England colonies. 

There, the people living together in small, compact communities, originated a local government which covered a smaller area—the TOWN GOVERNMENT (see page 102). In 

New England, class differences did not exist to such a great degree as they did in the South; hence, in New England, more people had a voice in the government. 

The idea of dividing the colonies into counties spread as the colonies grew in population and needed more governmental services and control. After the Revolutionary War, the county system of government spread westward and southward into the new states, and it remains today an important unit in our governmental make-up. (Refer again to the circle diagram on page 176, and get the place of 

COUNTY GOVERNMENT firmly fixed in your mind.) 

Counties vary greatly in size, in population, and in number in the various states. On maps of individual states, which you can find in your geography books, your school atlases, or encyclopedias, count the number of COUNTIES in some of the different states. 

The Purpose of County Government. County government exists for the welfare of the people who live within all the municipalities (towns, villages, and cities) within the COUNTY. It binds the municipalities together and exercises such control over them as is necessary for their common welfare.

The COUNTY GOVERNMENT is less important in the New 

England States than in any other section. There, the town (or township) government carries on the services that in other sections of the country are rendered by the counties. 

One must keep in mind that the COUNTY is subordinate to the state, just as the municipality is subordinate to the county. The county is created by the state; hence, it is subject to it. The county has only such power as is given to it by the state legislature or by the state constitution. 

(Re-read the discussion of the COUNTY in PART II, begin ning on page 36.) The state uses the COUNTY (as it sometimes uses the municipalities) to help carry out its laws and to collect its taxes. 

COUNTY HOME FOR THE AGED IN ALABAMA Some of the Common Functions of a County are: 

1. The collection of municipal, county, and state taxes. 

2. The care of the poor, the aged, the insane, the orphans; and the punishment of the less serious lawbreakers.

County Government 

163 3. The building and maintaining of roads and bridges. 

4. The keeping of important records, such as registering land titles and vital statistics (births, marriages, and deaths). 

5. The disposition of the property of deceased (dead) 

6. The general administration of state laws for the welfare of all concerned. Tum 

HOME FOR DEPENDENT CHILDREN, MILWAUKEE COUNTY, WISCONSIN The County Seat. Just as the Federal and state govern ments need buildings (capitols) to house the offices of their various officials, so the COUNTY needs a building to house its offices. The county seat is sometimes called the county court house, and is usually located in one of the large cities of the county. Many counties have built beautiful and imposing court houses.

COUNTY GENERAL HOSPITAL, MILWAUKEE COUNTY, WISCONSIN 

(See also pages 100 and 282.)

In order to render their services, COUNTIES often erect and maintain other buildings besides a court house: such as, an asylum, a poor house and farm, a hospital, and a jail. 

2. THE DEPARTMENTS OF COUNTY GOVERNMENT The Legislative Department. The legislative body¹ of 

COUNTIES varies in many ways throughout the country. 

Some states call it board of supervisors; others, board of commissioners. Alabama calls hers, court of county commissioners; Kentucky, fiscal court; New Jersey, chosen free holders; and Louisiana, police jury.

Not only is this body called by different names in different states, but it is also elected in different ways. In 'Called "legislative body" because in most states it may exercise legislative authority.

County Government 

165 some states, it is elected at large by the county. In others, it is elected by districts within the county. In still others, it is elected by the different municipalities within the county.

The county board also varies in size. In some states, it consists of only three members; in some states, five; in others, seven. In states where it is elected by municipalities (such as, Wisconsin and Michigan) the membership sometimes runs up to fifty or more.

Regardless of these differences, the power and duty of the COUNTY BOARD throughout the country are similar. 

Most states allow the COUNTY BOARD to fix a county tax rate and to spend county funds as it sees fit. Some states allow it to pass laws (ordinances) on matters of local concern. It has charge of all county buildings and other properties, and provides for their maintenance. Primarily, of course, the county board is an agent in administering state laws. 

LINN COUNTY JAIL, CEDAR RAPIDS, IOWA. For a U. S. Pen., see p. 240.

The Executive Department. The sheriff is one of the chief executive officers of the COUNTY; and, in most cases, he is elected by the voters of the county, usually for a term of two or four years. His duties vary in different states, but in general he is the chief police officer of the county. 

He makes arrests and holds the offender until the case is properly disposed of by a court. He has charge of the county jail. In large and populous counties, he usually has the power to appoint deputies to assist him. In case of serious trouble he may call on the governor for the services of the state militia. 

Another executive officer found in most COUNTIES, is the coroner. His duty is to hold inquests (official inquiries) on unusual or mysterious deaths and to investigate their causes, so that in case a crime has been committed, the criminal can be brought to justice. If necessary, he may draft a coroner's jury to help him decide on difficult cases. 

In counties where there are large cities, the coroner is an important officer. (Can you tell why?)

A register of deeds is a county officer in many states. 

His duty is to keep records of a vital nature in his county. 

He enters in large books (plat books) the ownership of all land within the county. He also keeps a record of legal documents, such as deeds and mortgages, and birth, marriage, and death certificates.

A county treasurer is found in most counties. His duty is, of course, similar to that of any treasurer. He collects and keeps all county funds and pays them out on the order of the proper authority. Like most important treasurers, he is placed under bond to protect the people's money against careless or dishonest handling.

In many counties there is a county clerk who acts as a sort of clerical officer. He issues licenses of various kinds; keeps many records; acts as an election officer where there is no special election commission; and keeps vital records where there is no REGISTER OF DEEDS. 

In all counties (except in the New England states) there

County Government 

167 is a county superintendent of schools who has general charge of the schools within the county. (See discussion under SCHOOLS on page 157.)

Other officials oftentimes found in COUNTY GOVERNMENT 

are: an attorney, usually called district attorney or county attorney, who acts as legal adviser for the county; a surveyor who surveys land for the county when necessary; an auditor who looks over the accounting books of all county officials, and keeps general supervision over the county's finances; a county highway commissioner who supervises the construction and maintenance of county roads and bridges; an income tax officer who levies and collects the state's income taxes; and a county agricultural agent who helps and advises farmers on agricultural subjects. 

These various COUNTY OFFICIALS have their office in the county court house. Some are elected by the voters, others appointed by some officer, board, or commission, for terms of various lengths. (See questions 5, 6, and 7 at the end of this UNIT concerning your COUNTY EXECUTIVE OFFICERS. 

The Judicial Department. The COUNTY is an important unit of government for judicial functions. (Re-read the purpose of judicial officers (judges) on page 43.) Some counties have a county judge who hears regular civil and criminal cases,¹ but usually a COUNTY JUDGE acts only as a probate judge, that is, he settles the estates of deceased people.

Most states have a system of district or circuit courts which are superior to the county courts. They are called 

DISTRICT or CIRCUIT COURTS because the state is divided into districts containing one or more counties. The district judge circulates about his district, holding court at certain stated times in the various county seats within his district. 

Some cases the judge tries alone; for others, he is required by law to draft the services of a jury to help him in his 'A civil case involves the protection of a citizen's private rights. 

A criminal case involves a wrong committed against an individual. 'A jury is a group of citizens, selected according to some lawful plan, who help a judge decide certain kinds of cases.

decision. CIRCUIT COURTS are really state courts, but they function through the counties within the circuits or dis tricts. COUNTY and CIRCUIT JUDGES are either elected by the voters of the unit they serve, or are appointed by the governor of the state.

In most counties there is a clerk of court who keeps all records of court cases tried within his county. The county or district attorney is also an important officer in judicial procedures, as he must bring offenders to trial. He is often called prosecuting attorney.

UNIT III. STATE GOVERNMENT THE PROBLEMS You have now learned some things about MUNICIPAL GOV- 

ERNMENTS, especially about the government of the municipality in which you live, and about the government of the COUNTY. These governments, however, are agents of the 

STATE GOVERNMENT. They get their power either by state laws or the state constitution. They exist to help carry out the laws of the state and the spirit of the provisions of the state constitution. The purpose of your state government is probably well expressed in the preamble of your state con stitution. In general, STATE GOVERNMENT exists to protect your life, liberty, and property, and to help you to attain happiness and security as a citizen of these United States. 

It is the purpose of this UNIT to make a study of the 

GOVERNMENT OF YOUR STATE interesting and understand able. It will help you answer such questions as: (1) How is my STATE governed? and (2) What services does my

STATE GOVERNMENT render to its people?

1. THE ORIGIN OF STATES 

The Early Colonies. From your study of American history, you have seen how the various colonies were settled along the Atlantic seaboard in colonial times. VIRGINIA was the first to be started at Jamestown, in 1607; and 

GEORGIA, the last, in 1732. These original colonies, thirteen in all, were governed according to a charter which had been granted to them by England. When the colonies became independent after the Revolutionary War, they became known as states, and have ever since been called such. (See the last paragraph of the Declaration of Independence. 

Note how the General Congress assembled, in 1776, refers to the colonies as the United States of America.)

MAP SHOWING BOUNDARIES OF REGIONS ACQUIRED BY THE UNITED 

STATES at various times from 1783 to 1853. See question 1, page 191.

Westward Growth of the Nation. 

After the Peace of Paris (1783), the Federal government asked the STATES to give up their land claims between the Appalachian Highlands and the Mississippi River. This they gradually did, and that land then became Federal property. Between 1783 and 1853, the Federal government acquired all the present domain (territory) of the United States. It was acquired

as follows: Louisiana Territory, 1803; Florida, 1819; 

Texas, 1845; Oregon, 1846; Mexican Cession, 1848; and 

Gadsden Purchase Territory, 1853. (See 1, page 191.)

How New States Originated. When the pioneers began to cross the mountains (Appalachian Highlands) and to settle in the territory which was no longer under the control of any STATE, the Federal government realized that it would have to provide a government for these sparsely settled regions. Accordingly, under the ARTICLES OF CON- 

FEDERATION in 1787, Congress provided a temporary government for one large tract of land, north of the Ohio River, south of the Great Lakes, and east of the Mississippi River. 

The law providing for the government over this region is commonly called the Northwest Ordinance. This ORDINANCE, however, made no provision for the land that the United 

States was in the future to acquire.

Whenever a new region began to be settled, Congress organized it into a UNIT called a territory; gave it a boundary; authorized the President to appoint a territorial governor and judges; and provided for the election of a territorial legislature. This gave the territory a temporary government. With the exception of the thirteen original states, Texas, and California,¹ all the STATES went through a period of territoryship.

Whenever the TERRITORY acquired sufficient population (enough to entitle it to a Representative in Congress) the voters of the territory were authorized by an Enabling Act (passed by Congress) to call a constitutional convention to draft a constitution for the prospective state. This constitution was then submitted to the voters of the territory for approval. If the voters approved it, the constitution was then submitted to Congress for final approval or rejection. 

If approved by Congress, the territory became a state, and it could immediately undertake to organize a regular STATE 

GOVERNMENT. The FIRST FIVE STATES to be ADMITTED to the 

'Find in your history books why Texas and California were not organized as territories before becoming states.

(1791); KENTUCKY (1791); TENNESSEE (1796); OHIO 

(1803); and LOUISIANA (1812). States were periodically admitted until 1912. The LAST THREE to enter the Union were OKLAHOMA (1907); NEW MEXICO (1912); and ARIZONA (1912).

2. STATE GOVERNMENTS BUILT UPON STATE CONSTITUTIONS All State Governments Are Very Similar. The govern ments of all the states are very similar. They may differ in details, but the fundamental principles of the governments are the same in all states. The fundamental law of each state is, of course, the state constitution. (Re-read the previous discussion on STATE CONSTITUTIONS beginning on page 55). Each STATE GOVERNMENT is built around the three great departments: legislative, executive, and judicial.

Passage from a State Constitution. The following passage from the STATE CONSTITUTION of Wisconsin, makes provi sion for the election of the legislature of that state:

ARTICLE IV LEGISLATIVE Legislative power. SECTION 1. The legislative power shall be vested in a senate and assembly.

Senate and assembly. SECTION 2. The number of the members of the assembly shall never be less than fifty-four nor more than one hundred. The senate shall consist of a number not more than one-third nor less than one-fourth of the number of the members of the assembly.

Apportionment. SECTION 3. (As amended November, 1910.) 

At their first session after each enumeration made by the authority of the United States, the legislature shall apportion and district anew the members of the senate and assembly, according to the number of inhabitants, excluding Indians not taxed, soldiers, and officers of the United States army and navy. 

Assemblymen, how chosen. SECTION 4. (As amended Novem ber, 1881.) The members of the assembly shall be chosen biennially, by single districts, on the Tuesday succeeding the first 

Monday of November after the adoption of this amendment, by the qualified electors of the several districts, such districts to be bounded by county, precinct, town or ward lines, to consist of contiguous territory and be in as compact form as practicable. 

Senators, how chosen. SECTION 5. (As amended November, 

1881.) The senators shall be elected by single districts of convenient contiguous territory, at the same time and in the same manner as members of the assembly are required to be chosen; and no assembly district shall be divided in the formation of a

State Government 

175 senate district. The senate districts shall be numbered in the regular series, and the senators shall be chosen alternately from the odd and even-numbered districts. The senators elected or holding over at the time of the adoption of this amendment shall continue in office till their successors are duly elected and qualified; and after the adoption of this amendment all senators shall be chosen for the term of four years.

Qualifications of members. SECTION 6. No person shall be eligible to the legislature who shall not have resided one year within the state, and be a qualified elector in the district which he may be chosen to represent. (See questions 3, 4, 5, 6, and 7 on page 191 regarding the

CONSTITUTION of your own state. Every student of civics should study and discuss those questions thoroughly.

3. THE IMPORTANCE OF STATE GOVERNMENT TO THE AMERICAN CITIZEN State Governments Are Superior to Municipal and County 

Governments. The government of the STATE is superior to all other governmental units within it, such as the coun-

ty, town, village, and city. STATE LAWS apply to all people within the state. The smaller units of government cannot make laws or exercise any power contrary to the state government. In fact, county and municipal governments are created by the STATE, and are at all times subject to the will of the STATE. They are really agents to help carry out state laws. They have no powers except those granted to them by the state.

State Government Affects Our Lives on All Sides. Mu nicipal and county governments affect our lives daily;

but, MILYERS

State Government 

177 in reality, they are only agents of the STATE. The STATE provides us with a system of education; defines our civil rights and privileges by its constitution; protects our life and property by its laws. The STATE Safeguards our health, and, through its agents, furnishes us with wholesome recreational facilities. In order to give us these services, the 

STATE collects from its citizens taxes and fees. 

4. THE STATE LEGISLATIVE DEPARTMENT Structure and Membership of State Legislatures. Each 

STATE has a legislative body composed of two parts or houses. In all states the UPPER HOUSE is called the Senate,¹ but the LOWER HOUSE has various names, such as, Assembly, General Assembly, Delegate Assembly, and House of 

Representatives. In all states, the SENATE is a smaller body than the lower house. The members of both houses are elected by districts. Each DISTRICT usually has about the same population, to make the representation equal. Sometimes a county is a district; sometimes a district is larger or smaller than a county. STATE LEGISLATORS, like all other legislators, are representatives of the people. (See page 

42 on legislative bodies.)

The term of office for state legislators varies in the different states. SENATORS, as a rule, serve longer terms than do members of the lower house. The terms vary in the different states from one to four years. The membership of both houses, the manner in which the members are elected, their term of office, their qualifications, and their salary are usually regulated by the state constitution. (See question 6, page 192.)

Powers of State Legislatures. The Constitution of the 

United States, by the Tenth Amendment, gives to states 'Do not confuse the state senate with the United States Senate. 

One can avoid confusion by always prefixing the word senate with either "state" or "United States." "Do not confuse a state house of representatives with the Federal House of Representatives. Use the same precaution as is suggested for the senate.

туз (and consequently to their LEGISLATURES) a large measure of power. Of course, the power of any state legislature is largely determined by the state constitution. You should study directly from your state constitution the powers given to your state legislature. You should also study SECTION 10 of ARTICLE I, and AMEND- MENT X of the CONSTITU- TION OF THE UNITED 

STATES, as these provisions deal with the powers of states.

Sessions of State Legis latures.

In a few states, the LEGISLATURE meets annually; but in most states, only biennially (or every other year). Most states elect their STATE

LEGISLATORS at the gen eral fall election in November, of even-numbered years. They then take office the following January, when the legislature convenes. (What are the constitutional provisions of your state for the sessions of your legislature, and for the election of its members?)

Organization of State Legislatures. Each house in the 

STATE LEGISLATURE has its own chamber in which to meet; and the two houses always meet separately, unless there is need for a joint session for same special purpose. In order to carry on its work smoothly and efficiently, each HOUSE requires the services of certain officers and the guidance of set rules. Probably the most important officer in each house 

MAP SHOWING THE STATE OF ILLI- NOIS DIVIDED INTO ITS STATE SENATORIAL DISTRICTS

State Government 

179 is the "chairman" or "presiding officer." In most states he is elected from the group after it convenes for a session; but in some states, the state constitution provides that the lieutenant-governor shall be the CHAIRMAN of the senate. 

The chairman (presiding officer) of the senate (the upper house) is usually called the president and of the house of representatives (the lower house) the speaker. 

Chamber of Commerce, Lincoln, Nebraska

THE NEW STATE CAPITOL AT LINCOLN, NEBRASKA 

(For picture of another State Capitol, see page 170.) 

In states where the chairman of the houses is elected by the houses, he is almost always a member of the political party which has the majority of members in the house concerned. Naturally, the majority group in a house will elect one of their own party to the chairmanship. This system allows political parties to have a great influence on state legislatures, because the chairman usually has important powers and much party influence. Each house has numerous other OFFICERS, such as, clerks and sergeants-at-arms who are necessary for orderly procedure of legislative work. 

Legislative Committees. Because of the large membership of the houses of a STATE LEGISLATURE, much of the

preparatory work on bills, which are to come up for consideration and possible passage into laws, is done by committees. In many states, the chairman of each house (that is, the president of the senate and the speaker) has great influence and power in appointing the various COMMITTEES. 

Hence, if he and his party do not favor a proposed bill, or for some reason do not wish it made into a law, he can appoint a committee whom he knows will be hostile to it, to investigate and consider the proposed legislation. The bill, then, will probably be "pigeon-holed"; or, the committee will bring in an unfavorable recommendation and thus influence a majority in the house to vote against it. Private individuals who want a bill passed or killed, usually work through the committee. Try to list the advantages and disadvantages of the committee system of investigating and considering bills. When you discuss this, keep the welfare of the people of the state in mind. Sometimes, when important bills are discussed in a committee, interested people are invited to attend and express their views. This is called a "public hearing" on a bill.

How Laws Are Made. The process of drawing up or writing (drafting) a bill so that it will be clear and simple, easy to understand, and difficult to misunderstand, is no easy task. In the past many state legislators found it difficult to get accurate information relating to bills (prospective laws) and to get the provisions of these bills into clear and understandable language. As a result of this difficulty and in the hope of framing more exact laws, many states have established libraries in their capitol buildings. In these libraries are trained workers who help legislators get necessary and useful information, and who assist in the drafting of bills. Such libraries are usually called "Legislative Reference Bureaus." More than thirty states now have such libraries in one form or another. 

After a BILL has been prepared, it is introduced into one of the houses of the legislature and is usually referred immediately to a committee for investigation and considera-

State Government 

181 tion. Hundreds of bills, the good with the bad, never get beyond the COMMITTEE. They are "smothered," "shelved," "put to sleep," or "pigeon-holed" as the sayings go. If the committee report is favorable, the bill is then brought before the entire house to be discussed¹ and finally voted upon. 

If passed by a majority² vote, it is sent to the other house where it goes through a similar process. If the second house passes it, the bill is sent to the governor for his approval. If he signs it, the measure becomes a law. The time it is to go into effect and be enforced is stated in the bill. If the governor vetoes³ (disapproves) it, however, he states why he does so in a written veto message and sends the bill back along with the veto message to the house where it originated. There it either dies or is taken up again. If the authors of the vetoed measure feel that enough legislators will support and vote for it, they start it through the process of passage a second time. If, on this second effort, it passes both houses by a sufficiently large majority (usually two-thirds) the bill becomes a law without the governor's approval. This process of legislation is called. "over-riding the executive veto" or "passing a law over the executive's head." The new law is now put on the state statute books, and the proper officers proceed to enforce its provisions. (You should study your own state constitution to find out how bills may originate and become laws in your state legislature.) 4 The Practice of Lobbying. It has become quite a common practice of corporations, organizations, unions, private in- 'After being discussed, amendments may be added to the bill, which may change it in various ways. 

The state legislatures differ in the number of votes necessary to pass various kinds of legislation, especially in regard to passing bills over the governor's veto. Constitutional provisions in the various states also differ in this regard. You should consult your own state constitution on these points. 3 

See footnote, page 184. 'The statutes are the laws of the political division of government. 

There are state statutes which are the acts of the state legislature and Federal statutes which are acts of Congress. A statute book is a book containing copies of laws.

dividuals, and special groups of people promoting all sorts of interests to send personal representatives to the state capitol¹ during legislative sessions. These people try to influence legislation by inducing the legislators to introduce certain bills or to modify or vote against certain pending · measures. People who are thus engaged are called lobbyists, and their efforts to influence or direct legislation is called lobbying. LOBBYISTS are not allowed on the floor of a legislative chamber; but they often do their work of influencing in the lobbies, ante-rooms, or corridors of the legislative chambers. They are occasionally invited to appear before 

COMMITTEES. Oftentimes their work is secretly done. LOB- 

BYISTS, of course, work primarily for the passage of laws which will benefit those individuals or groups which they represent, and for the defeat of bills which will hamper their interests. Oftentimes there are selfish motives back of lobbying. (See question 10, page 192.) On the other hand, the LOBBYIST may be, and frequently is, a specialist who can give valuable and helpful advice to the legislative committee that is considering important legislation. 

5. THE STATE EXECUTIVE DEPARTMENT The State Governor and His Powers. The constitution of each state provides for a governor who acts as the state's chief executive. He is elected by the voters of the state for a term of two to four years.2 In colonial times, the ROYAL 

GOVERNOR had to carry out the wishes of the king. In so doing, he oftentimes became a much disliked and unpopular official in the colonies. Because of this feeling of dislike of colonial governors, the first state constitutions were inclined to give STATE GOVERNORS little power. This feeling has gradually disappeared, however, until today most state constitutions give the governor liberal powers. Today, 'Lobbying is probably carried on in Congress at Washington, D. C., to an even greater degree than it is in the state legislatures. Do you think it is also practiced in city and county law-making bodies? 

2In about one-half of the states his term is two years; in the others four years, except in New Jersey which is three.

State Government 

183 rather than being one who is feared or looked upon with suspicion, he is regarded as the one who protects the people's rights. As the chief executive of the state, he is pledged to enforce the laws of the state; he is guardian of the people's liberty. As such, his powers can be classified, in most states, as: EXECUTIVE, LEGISLATIVE, and JUDI- CIAL. 

The Governor's Executive Powers. As chief executive of the state, the governor must execute (enforce) the laws of the state. In most states, however, other EXECUTIVE 

OFFICERS are elected, such as, a secretary of state, a state treasurer, and an attorney general, who have specific executive duties to perform. These officers must cooperate and work with the GOVERNOR, if the executive department of a state's government is to run smoothly. 

The GOVERNOR is commander-in-chief of the state militia. 

At his command, the militia may be used to enforce laws and to keep peace. Some states have a system of state police who are under the charge of the governor. Many state constitutions allow the governor to make appointments of state officials, boards, and commissions.

The Governor's Legislative Powers. One of the governor's legislative powers is his privilege to make recommendations through messages to the legislature. A strong governor keeps in close touch with the legislature, and through forceful MESSAGES urges recommendations as to what laws should be considered. Much of the governor's success in the legislative field will depend upon the coöperation that he gets from the legislature itself. The legislature, of course, may or may not follow his recommendations and suggestions.

If a governor happens to be of one political party, and the majority of the legislature of another party with opposing views, he usually cannot hope for much coöperation. 

Another power of the governor, which may be classed as legislative, is that of calling special sessions of the state legislature. If the governor feels that some matter needs immediate attention, he may call the legislature together

Chamber of Commerce, Albany, N. Y.

GOVERNOR'S MANSION (OFFICIAL RESIDENCE), ALBANY, NEW YORK 

Some states provide a residence for the governor. for such special purpose. This is often done, since most state legislatures meet in regular session only once in every two years. (Has your state legislature held a special session recently? If so, for what purpose?)

In all states except NORTH CAROLINA and RHODE ISLAND, the governor has in the veto¹ a power which may be called legislative. This power allows him to veto (disapprove) any law which he deems unwise. This power, of course, may be over-ridden by the legislature. (See discussion on 

HOW LAWS ARE MADE on page 180.) 

The Governor's Judicial Powers. In most states, the 

GOVERNOR has some judicial powers, such as shortening jail sentences; lessening other forms of punishment; and pardoning and paroling prisoners. This power is usually given 'Veto comes from the Latin language, meaning "I forbid."

State Government 

185 to governors so that if punished people are later found to be innocent, or to be less guilty than originally adjudged, there will be some way to lessen their punishment or to pardon them completely. In most states there is a pardon board on whom the governor relies for advice in the treatment of people convicted of crime. 

Other Executive Officers: Most states elect a lieutenant governor at the same time, and for the same term, as the governor. He automatically takes over the governor's office in case that office, for any reason, becomes vacant. In most states, the LIEUTENANT-GOVERNOR presides over the state 

Senate when it is in session. 

The secretary of state has various duties in the different states. In general, he is the official record keeper for the state. All official papers and documents are handled through his office.

The state treasurer is the official money-keeper for the state. He collects all state taxes, licenses, and fees; and pays out, on order from the proper officials, all state money. 

The state auditor or comptroller keeps watch over the income and expenditure of state money. He helps plan budgets for the various departments of state government and estimates incomes and expenditures. He approves of all claims for money against the state treasury. As will be inferred, his work is closely connected with that of the 

TREASURER. 

The attorney-general is the lawyer for the state. He represents the state in all legal matters, and gives legal advice to all departments of state government. 

The state superintendent of schools is the head of the state school system. He has general supervisory charge of all the public schools of the state. (See discussion of this officer under SCHOOLS, page 152.)

In most states, these EXECUTIVE OFFICERS are elected by the voters of the state for a term which is the same as the governor's.

State Boards and Commissions. Every state government

has a great number of boards and commissions, many of which are appointed by the governor. Their purpose is to help carry out and enforce the state laws; to manage and direct state institutions and state enterprises; to control public utilities, etc. The number and kind of such BOARDS vary in the different states. If you were to walk through the corridors of some of the state capitol buildings, you would see on the office doors such signs as: Board of Charitable and Correctional Institutions; Board of Agriculture; 

Board of Health; Highway Commission; Banking Commission; Insurance Commission; Board of Medical Examiners; 

Conservation Commission. Each BOARD or COMMISSION is headed by a chief, a superintendent, or a chairman. Each has a corps of workers who help to carry out the duties of the board. 

6. THE STATE JUDICIAL DEPARTMENT Judicial Systems Vary in Different States. (Re-read the discussion beginning on page 43 on the Purpose of Courts and How the Three Departments of Government Work 

Together in Enforcing Laws.)

Each state has its own judicial (court) system, and they are all similar. Each state constitution either provides for a 

STATE JUDICIAL SYSTEM or allows the state legislature to create one.

Towns, villages, and cities have justices of the peace who are judicial officers for those municipalities. They try minor (petty) offenses and mete out justice on matters of a less serious nature. Counties sometimes have county judges who are empowered to try certain specified types of cases. 

The jurisdiction (power or scope) of all courts within a state is usually defined in the state constitution or by legis lative act. MUNICIPAL (page 106) and COUNTY COURTS 

(page 167) have already been discussed in this book under those political divisions.) 

Special Courts. To handle the many judicial (court) problems which arise, most states have created special courts,

especially in the large cities. These are called by various names, and their jurisdiction varies in the different states. 

Common SPECIAL COURTS are: police courts, district courts, orphans' courts, courts of small claims, and juvenile courts.

State Circuit Courts. 

Many states have a system of courts commonly called circuit courts. The state is divided into districts, and each district has a judge who holds court at stated times and places, within his district; he makes "circuits" about his district. These circuit judges are usually elected by the voters of the district (or circuit) for a term of years. 11 119 15 16 5TO 18 20 12 13 1/3 1

State Supreme Courts. 

All states have a COURT OF LAST RESORT (APPEAL) which is called by such names as court of appeals, supreme court of appeals or merely state supreme court. It consists of several judges (usually from six to ten) and hears only the more serious cases which have been appealed from the decision of lower courts. It also decides on the constitutionality of state laws.

Rank of State Courts. STATE COURTS can be ranked ac cording to their power or jurisdiction. The state constitution (or the state legislature) usually defines the jurisdiction of the various courts within a state. Under the laws of most states, when either party involved in a court case is not satisfied with the decision of a lower court, he may "appeal" to a higher court. DIAGRAM SHOWING THE RANK OF COURTS IN MOST STATES Juries and How They Are Selected. A petit jury is a body of men (or women) selected by a lawful plan to assist a judge in making his decision about a case. PETIT JURIES 

usually consist of twelve persons selected from lists of voters in the municipality where the court is held. A jury commission draws names from a box until twelve are drawn who are acceptable to both parties concerned in the trial. 

A person who is related to either party in the suit, or is in any other way interested in the case, is not an acceptable juror. Sometimes many names are drawn by the JURY COM- 

MISSIONERS before twelve acceptable JURORS are found. 

Trial by jury is one of the fundamental rights of Ameri can citizens. (See ARTICLE VI under AMENDMENTS in the FEDERAL CONSTITUTION.)

whether or not there is enough evidence against an accused person to hold him for a formal trial at court. GRAND 

JURIES are usually larger than petit ("little") juries, but are chosen in a similar way. (See ARTICLE V under AMEND- 

MENTS in the FEDERAL CONSTITUTION.) 

How a Court Works. When one breaks a law or is accused of breaking a law, he may be arrested by a proper officer. 

If one is not caught in the act of breaking a law, he cannot be arrested without a warrant from the proper authority, usually the attorney for the municipality concerned. (See 

ARTICLE IV under AMENDMENTS in the CONSTITUTION.) 

Of course, if one is caught in the act of breaking a law, he can be arrested without a warrant.

When taken to court, the accused is given a preliminary hearing and is allowed an opportunity of defense. If the judge believes him innocent, he is set free. If the evidence in the preliminary hearing indicates guilt, however, the accused is bound over for trial, and is either held in jail, or is allowed to go free on bail¹ until he can be given a trial. 

If he pleads not guilty, the accused secures or is furnished the services of an attorney to defend him at his trial. 

(Again, see ARTICLE VI under AMENDMENTS in the FED- ERAL CONSTITUTION.) 

Sometimes trials last for days and many witnesses are subpoenaed (called to court) to be questioned on what they know about the case. Opposing counsel (lawyers) fight hard for the welfare of their clients. At the conclusion of the trial, after all evidence has been presented and all witnesses have testified, and the lawyers have made their pleas (closing addresses), the judge "charges" the jury (that is, gives them instruction as to their legal duty). He then orders the jury to retire to a room, consider the evidence, and bring in a verdict. If the jury's verdict (decision) is 

NOT GUILTY, the accused is freed. If the verdict is GUILTY, 

¹Bail (or bond) is money or property pledged by the accused, or by his friends, to insure his appearance at trial at a given time. The amount of bond is fixed by the judge.

State Government 

191 the judge pronounces sentence and fixes the penalty which the law prescribes.

UNIT IV. FEDERAL (OR NATIONAL) GOVERNMENT THE PROBLEMS The FEDERAL (OR NATIONAL) GOVERNMENT is the govern ment of the entire nation. The Federal Constitution and the Federal laws are the highest laws of the land. Nearly one hundred twenty-five millions of people obey them and receive the benefits and protection which they afford. When our FEDERAL GOVERNMENT originated, at the close of the Revolutionary War, it was a new venture. It was framed and put into operation by such statesmen as GEORGE WASH- 

INGTON, ALEXANDER HAMILTON, JOHN ADAMS, and THOMAS 

JEFFERSON.

Through the years it has been guided by such heroes as ANDREW JACKSON, ABRAHAM LINCOLN, THEODORE ROOSEVELT, and WOODROW WILSON. For nearly a century and a half it has stood the test. The future of our government will depend upon you and your fellow classmates throughout the country. Study it, and be prepared!

It is the purpose of this UNIT to acquaint you with the 

MACHINERY of our FEDERAL GOVERNMENT. It will help you answer such questions as: (1) How has our FEDERAL

GOVERNMENT developed? (2) How is it organized today? 

and (3) What services does it offer to us? UNITED

1. THE ORIGIN AND PURPOSE OF OUR FEDERAL GOVERNMENT 

The Articles of Confederation and the Constitution of the United States. While the thirteen original colonies were struggling for independence in the Revolutionary War, they gradually united into a CONFEDERATION of States. 

This first union of the states was accomplished in a document called the Articles of Confederation. This instrument proved totally inadequate as a basis for a FEDERAL GOVERN- MENT, and it was soon supplanted by the Constitution of the United States. (Re-read the story of the origin of the 

ARTICLES OF CONFEDERATION and the CONSTITUTION OF THE UNITED STATES in PART II, UNIT II of this book, beginning on page 58.)

The FEDERAL CONSTITUTION is the basis of our Federal government. It is the fundamental law of the entire nation.

)

Furthermore, it is the highest law of the land. (Refer to the circle diagram on page 176, which shows how the FED- ERAL CONSTITUTION encircles all other laws, codes, charters, and constitutions.) The Purpose of Federal Government. The Federal gov ernment is the government of, by, and for the entire nation.

Just as the COUNTY GOVERNMENT is concerned with the entire county, and the STATE GOVERNMENT with the entire state, so the FEDERAL GOVERNMENT is concerned with the whole nation. Its laws affect all the people of the Nation. 

It controls our relations, as a Nation, with the other nations of the world. It attempts "... to form a more perfect union, establish justice, insure domestic tranquillity, provide for the common defense, promote the general welfare, and secure the blessings of liberty to ourselves and our posterity

2. THE FEDERAL LEGISLATIVE DEPARTMENT The Congress. The Federal LEGISLATIVE BODY is called the Congress.

members in the House of Representatives and 96 Senators in the United States Senate. Representatives are chosen in proportion to the population of each state; but there are only two Senators from each state. The framers of the 

Constitution created the CONGRESS with two houses, so that one branch could act as a check on the other, and thereby prevent the hasty passage of Federal laws, and make dif- ¹See footnotes on page 177. Never confuse a state senate with the

When Congress Meets. Before the Twentieth Amend ment was added to the Constitution, Congress met once each year, beginning on the first Monday in December. 

(See ARTICLE I, SECTION 4.) The long session (or first ses sion) began in odd-numbered years and lasted until sometime in the following spring or summer. The long session had no definite date for ending or adjourning. The short session (or second session) began in even-numbered years and had a definite date for adjourning-March 4th. The two sessions are called a Congress, and the Congresses have

been numbered consecutively since the first one met in 1789. The first two sessions were called the first Congress; the second two sessions, the second Congress; and so on through the years. The session which convened on the first 

Monday in December, 1931, was the first (or long) session of the 72nd Congress; and the one which convened on the first Monday in December, 1932, was the second (or short) session of the 72nd Congress. (See diagram on page 199.)

The Short Session and the Objections to It. Throughout the country, the members of Congress (United States Senators and Representatives) are usually elected at the fall (or 

November) elections, in even-numbered years. Before the 

20th Amendment was added to the Constitution, the Congressmen (Senators and Representatives) who were elected in November did not take office until the following March 

4th. Not all sitting members of Congress are re-elected when their terms expire; hence, members who were defeated in the fall elections had to attend the short session because their newly-elected successors did not take office until the following March 4th, after the short session closed. These defeated members who had to represent their states and districts were called "Lame Ducks," and the short session was often called the "Lame Duck session.”

They were given this name because they had to represent the voters who had rejected them, and because they were ' often accused of just sitting in their seats to fulfill the law without taking an active part in the work of the Congress. 

The last "Lame Duck" session, which convened the first 

Monday in December, 1932, contained over 150 "Lame Ducks." 'In the early days of our government under the Constitution, it was thought that there should be a long time (approximately four months) between the election of our Congressmen and President, and their inauguration. Hence, the elections were held in the fall (usually November), and the inauguration was the following

March 4th. This was done because of the extremely slow methods of communication and transportation in the early days. In the early days of the Republic, it would have been quite impossible for newlyelected Congressmen from distant states to get to Washington for a Congressional session in just a few weeks time.

Federal Government 

199 

The Twentieth Amendment. Today, with our rapid means of transportation and communication, it is no longer necessary to have a period of four months elapse between our Congressional elections and the time when the elected members take office. For many years there was a feeling on the part of some national leaders that the old system should be changed; but the movement made little headway until recent years. On March 2, 1932, Congress submitted the pro posed Twentieth Amendment, sponsored by Senator George 

Norris of Nebraska, to the states to be ratified. By the middle of January, 1933, enough states (36) had ratified 1934 1935

it to make it a part of the Constitution. The TWENTIETH 

AMENDMENT provides that members elected to either branch of Congress, in the fall elections of any year, take office the following January 3rd, when the first session begins. Also, that the President, elected in November, take office the following January (instead of March 4). This new scheme does away with the long wait between the Congressional and Presidential elections and their inaugurations, and with the undesirable short session (Lame Duck session) of Congress. The first session of the 73rd Congress convened on January 3, 1934, and the first presidential inauguration under the new plan was on January 

20, 1937. (See diagram above.)

The President may call an extra (or special) session of Congress (when it is not in session) whenever he feels that there is urgent need for such a session. For example, President Wilson called a SPECIAL SESSION of Congress in 

April, 1917, when relations between Germany and the United States became very strained. The result of that special session was that Congress, at the request of Presi dent Wilson, declared war on Germany. (See ARTICLE II, SECTION 3.) The Federal House of Representatives: How Elected. 

Members of the House are chosen in the ratio of the population of each state to the United States, but each state, under the Constitution, is entitled to at least one Repre sentative. (See ARTICLE I, SECTION 2.) Congress decides how large the House is to be. A Federal CENSUS is taken every ten years to determine the population of the entire United States. In 1910, Congress decided that the size of the House should be 435, and to date that number has not been changed. The Federal census of 1930 showed the population of the United States to be 122,775,046. When that (population) figure is divided by 435, the result is approximately 282,000, which represents the number of people re quired for ONE REPRESENTATIVE in the House. That figure will hold until the next Federal census is taken in 1940. 

The process of determining how many people one Representative (usually called Congressman) is to represent, is called apportionment.

After the number of people has been determined which one Representative must represent, the total population of each state is divided by that number. The result, of course, is the number of Representatives that that state is entitled to for the following ten-year period, or until another Federal census is taken. For example, WISCONSIN in 1930 had a total population of 2,939,006. That figure divided by 

282,000 gives 10+. Hence, for the decade between 1930 

and 1940, WISCONSIN is entitled to ten Representatives in the Federal House of Representatives.

Federal Government 

201 In 1842, Congress passed an act which says that Representatives must be elected by districts within the several states "composed of contiguous and compact territory and containing as nearly as practicable an equal number of inhabitants." After the APPORTIONMENT has been completed every ten years, each state legislature must divide. its state into DISTRICTS to conform to the above law. This process is called districting, or re-districting, as the case may be. These districts are called Congressional Districts, because each elects a Representative (Congressman) to represent it in the lower house of Congress. 1 10 2
