MAP SHOWING A GERRY- MANDERED STATE How the House Is Organized. Principal Officers. If it is to accomplish work, an unwieldy body of 435 members naturally needs some kind of organization and some rules of regulation and procedure. 'Sometimes in the past, corrupt and unfair practices have been resorted to in making such legislative districts, both state and Federal. In order to elect a member of a particular political party to the legislative body concerned, districts have purposely been made into odd, irregular, twisted shapes, so as to group within it a preponderance of that political party. This corrupt practice in setting district boundaries is called gerrymandering. Find in your histories or encyclopedia why it is called by that particular name.

The principal officers of the House are: Speaker, Clerk, Sergeant-at-arms, Door-keeper, Chaplain, and Postmaster.

As the term of office of Representatives is only two years, the House must organize anew every second session, or when a new Congress begins.

The Speaker is the most influential officer of the House. 

He is its chairman or presiding officer. A member of the 

​From an Old Steel Engraving THE OLD FEDERAL HOUSE OF REPRESENTATIVES 

(See also page 216.)

House is elected to the SPEAKERSHIP by the House. He is, of course, one of the popular members of the majority party in that body. Sometime before the opening meeting of a new Congress, the members of each party meet in caucus and select their candidates for the Speakership and other offices. In the formal election which follows, the majority party elects its candidate for SPEAKER and then proceeds to organize the House.

Federal Government 

203 

The Power of the Speaker. Before 1911, the SPEAKER 

had greater power than today. He could appoint practically all Committees, and since Congress functions through Committees, the Speaker's control over Committees gave him great control over legislation. If some law were proposed which the Speaker did not approve of, he could refer it to a Committee which would refuse to "report it out." Hence, the bill would never come before the entire House for a vote. In 1911, however, the rules were changed; and now most Committees are appointed by a "Committee on Committees" which is elected largely by the majority party. 

This reform greatly diminished the power of the Speaker. 

The SPEAKER still is, however, a most important and influential officer in the House. He still refers bills to Committees. He has power to recognize or not to recognize those who wish to speak from the floor. And, of course, being the recognized leader of the majority party in the House, he is in a position to exercise authority on all members of his party. His influence is sought in pushing legislation through Congress, and his is an important voice in the party councils.

The Committee System. The House is a large body, and every member has a right to introduce as many bills as he pleases. Oftentimes thousands of bills are introduced in a single session. One can readily see that the House would get nowhere if all members were required, or even tried, to study, discuss, and vote intelligently on all bills introduced. Consequently a system of Committees has grown up, and most of the work of Congress is carried on in these COMMITTEES. There are about fifty regular Committees in the House and, as the need arises, special Committees are appointed. A Committee on Ways and Means considers all bills pertaining to taxes and money raising; a Committee on Appropriations has the responsibility for providing for the appropriation of money for the various Departments of the Federal government. Then, there is a Committee on 

Postal Affairs; Indian Affairs; Foreign Affairs; Naval

Affairs; Military Affairs; and many others. COMMITTEES 

vary in size. They usually number somewhere between three and twenty members. As bills are introduced, they are referred by the Speaker to the proper Committee for study and recommendation. Hundreds of bills never get beyond the COMMITTEE. They are "killed" or "smothered" by an unfavorable Committee report, or by no report at all. 

The House usually trusts its COMMITTEES, and acts according to their reports. Committee meetings, as a rule, are held in secret, but sometimes "open hearings" are held, at which time those interested in the measure (bill) under consideration may present their views. 

Exclusive Powers of the House. The HOUSE has three important powers which the Senate does not have: First, all bills regarding taxation and the raising of money must originate in the House; on the theory that the House is more closely in touch with all the people than is the Senate. 

(See ARTICLE I, SECTION 7.) Second, the House has the power to elect the President, if the Electoral College (see page 213) fails to do so. (See ARTICLE XII of the AMEND- 

MENTS.) Third, the House may bring impeachment charges against any civil officer of the United States. If the charges are sustained, the officers so impeached may be expelled by two-thirds vote of the Senate. (See ARTICLE II, SECTION 4.) 

(Before leaving your study of the HOUSE, you should thor oughly study ARTICLE I, SECTION 2, and be able to answer questions 4 to 9, inclusive, on page 209.)

The United States Senate: How Elected. Term of Office. 

Two Senators from each state in the Union make up the United States Senate. Before 1913, they were elected by the legislatures of the various states; but since then, they have been chosen by direct vote of the people of the states. 

(See ARTICLE I, SECTION 3, CLAUSE 1, and ARTICLE XVII of the AMENDMENTS.) 

Unlike Representatives, SENATORS serve a term of six years, and their terms are so arranged that only one-third of the members' terms expire at the same time. (See ARTI-

Federal Government 

205 

CLE I, SECTION 3, CLAUSE 2.) This makes the Senate a continuous body. Its members are never all new at any one time.

Organization of the United States Senate. The UNITED 

STATES SENATE is a much smaller body than the House, having at the present time only 96 members, but its organization for business is similar. It has officers and Committees similar to those of the House. Its organization differs, however, in one respect; the presiding officer of the 

Senate is the Vice-President of the United States. He is not a member of the Senate and votes only in case of a tie. 

The Senate elects one of its own members to act as President pro tempore (temporary president) to take the Vice- President's place as presiding officer, should the Vice-President be absent, or should he succeed to the office of Presi dent. (See ARTICLE I, SECTION 3, CLAUSES 4 and 5.) Powers of the United States Senate. The powers of the 

SENATE may be divided into three distinct classes, namely, legislative, executive, and judicial: a. The Senate's legislative powers are equal to those of the House, except that no revenue bill (taxing or money-raising bill) can originate in the Senate. The 

Senate can amend taxation (revenue) bills, however. 

No bills can become laws without passing both Houses. The process of passing a bill through the 

Senate is similar to that in the House. b. The Senate's executive authority consists of its power to ratify treaties, and to confirm appointments made by the President. NO TREATY with a foreign nation, made by the President or his Commissioners, is valid until it has been RATIFIED by a two-thirds vote of the Senators present. A quorum¹ must be present in order to transact business. 

Likewise, the Senate must CONFIRM high official appointments of the President, such as Ambassa- 1 ¹A quorum is a majority-one over one-half. Forty-nine constitutes a quorum in the United States Senate.

dors, Ministers, Consuls, and Supreme Court Judges.

Only a majority vote of the Senate is required to confirm appointments. (See ARTICLE II, SECTION 2, CLAUSE 2.) c. The UNITED STATES SENATE may also exercise judi cial powers. It may act as a court of impeachment and try cases which have been brought by the House. In such cases, the Senators really become judges. They decide "guilty" or "not guilty" on the charges brought by the House. A two-thirds vote of the Senate is required to convict in any impeachment case. In case of conviction the only penalty the Senate can impose on the accused is to remove him from office and disqualify him from ever holding any office of trust, honor, or profit with the 

United States again. However, the offender may later be brought to trial in a regular court of law and further punished. Look up the story of the impeachment and trial of PRESIDENT ANDREW JOHN- 

SON, and report to your class on it. (See ARTICLE I,

SECTION 3, CLAUSES 6 and 7.) 

Senatorial Courtesy. Senatorial courtesy refers to the practice of United States Senators of supporting each other in approving or disapproving appointments made by the President. Since our country has grown so large and populous, and since Presidential appointments have so vastly increased, the Chief Executive relies more and more on the advice of Senators in making Federal appointments in the various states. Should the President exercise his right to appoint to a certain position someone other than the person recommended by a Senator from that state, the Senators, through what is known as SENATORIAL COURTESY, will re fuse to confirm the appointment. This custom makes it practically compulsory for the President to follow the advice and recommendations of the Senators, in making Federal appointments in the various states.

At the present time it is $10,000 per year for both Senators and Representa- tives, with extra allowances for mileage (in going to and from their homes to sessions) for clerk hire, and for other miscellaneous expenses, such as stationery, telegrams, etc.¹

Powers of Congress. The framers of the Constitution very carefully defined the powers of Congress; and to make sure that Congress could exercise all due power in making the nation's laws, they included an "elastic clause" (implied powers) which gives it great freedom in interpreting its powers, as set down by the Constitution. 'All Federal officials had salary cuts during the depression years.

You should carefully study and discuss (directly from the Constitution) the powers GIVEN to Congress by it. You should also study the powers that are FORBIDDEN to Con gress by the Constitution. (See ARTICLE I, SECTIONS 8 and 

9.)

How Federal Laws Are Made by Congress. The passage of bills through Congress is very similar to the passage of bills through any state legislature. (See discussion on How

STATE LAWS ARE MADE, beginning on page 180.) 

Any Congressman may introduce a bill, but important measures are usually introduced by Committee Chairmen. 

During a recent Congress over 24,000 bills were introduced in the two houses, but only slightly more than six per cent became laws. 2 When a bill is introduced into either house, it is immediately referred to the proper Committee. If the Committee reports favorably on it, the bill is discussed briefly, or at length,¹ in the house in which it originated. If it is passed by a majority vote of a quorum, it is sent to the other house where it goes through a similar process. If the second house passes the measure, it is sent to the President. If he signs it, the bill then becomes a law. If he disapproves of it, he vetoes (that is, does not sign it) and returns the unsigned bill to the house in which it originated, along with his veto message in which he states why he did not approve it.

The bill may be dropped at this point, or it may be taken up by both houses again. If both houses pass it a second time by a two-thirds vote, it becomes a law over the President's veto, and without his signature. 

The Constitution gives the President ten days (Sunday 'Sometimes a bill is discussed at great length before an entire house just to keep a vote from being taken on it. This is called filibustering. This practice is especially resorted to just before the close of a session to prevent a bill from being voted upon before adjournment, thereby killing it for that session. It can be reintroduced at the next session. "A quorum in either house of Congress, is one more than one-half its members.

Federal Government 

209 excepted) in which to sign or to veto a bill. If he disregards it, or more than ten days have elapsed, the bill automatically becomes a law. If Congress adjourns before the ten-day period is up, the President may ignore the bill and thereby kill it. This is called a pocket veto, as the President is said "to pocket the bill."

You should carefully study directly from the Constitu tion (ARTICLE I, SECTION 7) how bills may become laws. 

Each house has its own detailed rules on the introduction and passage of bills, but the above descriptions hold for the general routine. Each house is required to keep a journal of its daily action, known as the Congressional Record, which the public is entitled to see and read. (See ARTICLE I, SECTION 5, CLAUSES 3 and 4.)

3. THE FEDERAL EXECUTIVE DEPARTMENT 

The President.

"The executive power shall be vested in a President of the United States of America."-ARTICLE II, SECTION 1, CLAUSE 1. The President is the most powerful and conspicuous officer in our entire governmental make-up. 

He stands at the head of our American government. He represents the Nation as its Chief Executive. The Constitu- 

Brown Brothers THE WHITE HOUSE, WASHINGTON, D. C. 

The Official Residence of the President of the United States. (See also picture on page 245. For pictures of other Federal buildings, see pages 31, 33, 46, 97, 197, 226, 240, 285.) tion gives him many and varied powers. He is not only the leader of his political party, but is also the civil leader of over 122 millions of American people. When taking over the duties of his office he takes this oath: "I do solemnly swear (or affirm) that I will faithfully execute the office of President of the United States, and will, to the best of

my ability, preserve, protect, and defend the Constitution of the United States."-ARTICLE II, SECTION 1, CLAUSE 8. 

The President's Qualifications and Term of Office. The 

PRESIDENT must be a natural born citizen, thirty-five years of age, and he must have been a resident within the United 

States for fourteen years previous to his election. (ARTICLE II, SECTION 1, CLAUSE 5.) 

His term of office is four years, but there are no restrictions on the number of times he may be re-elected. However, it has become one of the "unwritten laws" of the land that no President be elected more than twice. (ARTICLE 

II, SECTION 1, CLAUSE 1.) 

The President's Salary. Congress fixes the salary of the 

PRESIDENT. It has changed through the years. From WASH- 

INGTON'S time up to 1873, it was $25,000 annually. From 

1873 to 1909, it was $50,000 annually. Since 1909, it has been $75,000 a year, plus $25,000 for traveling expenses and official entertainment. He has the use of the White 

House as his residence during his term of office. Congress also appropriates funds for the care of the White House, the maintenance of the Executive Offices in the White House, and for police protection for the President. (AR- 

TICLE II, SECTION 1, CLAUSE 7.) The Vice-President: Vacancies. It is the duty of the 

Vice-President to take the office of President whenever the President, because of removal, death, resignation, or inability, cannot discharge the duties of the office. 

Hence, the VICE-PRESIDENT must have the same qualifications as the President, and he is elected in the same way. Should both the President and the Vice-President for any reason become incapable of holding the office of President in the same term of office, the various members of the CABINET succeed to the office of President in the following order:¹ (1) Secretary of State; (2) Secretary of the Treasury; (3) Secretary of War; (4) Attorney-General; 'This provision was made by Congress, under Constitutional authority, in 1886. It is commonly called the Presidential Succession 

Act.

Federal Government 

213 (5) Postmaster-General; (6) Secretary of the Navy; (7) 

and Secretary of the Interior. (ARTICLE II, SECTION 1, 

CLAUSE 6.) The remaining three Cabinet offices did not exist in 1886, hence they were not included in the Sucession Act.

How the President Is Elected. When the Constitution was made, it was thought that the PRESIDENT and the VICE- PRESIDENT should not be chosen directly by the general voting public, but indirectly by a group of selected men, called Presidential Electors.¹ (ARTICLE II, SECTION 1, 

CLAUSE 2.) The framers of the Constitution thought that such a well-selected group of PRESIDENTIAL ELECTORS Would be ably fitted to make a wise choice in the selection of a President and a Vice-President. But the framers did not foresee the origin and growth of political parties and the effect that these were to have on the future political life of the nation. (Re-read the discussion on the ORIGIN AND 

GROWTH OF POLITICAL PARTIES beginning on page 71, and on the WORK OF THE NATIONAL NOMINATING CONVENTION 

on page 74.)

In the election of President and Vice-President, the "letter of the law," but not the "spirit of the law," is still car ried out as set down in the Constitution. (See ARTICLE II, SECTION 1, CLAUSE 3, and ARTICLE XII of the AMENDMENTS. 

Note, of course, that the latter replaces the former.)

Since the origin of political parties, the ELECTORS are merely the mouthpiece of the victorious party after each 

Presidential election. 

Today, in each state, the Electors are nominated by state party conventions. They are elected at large by the voters of each state at the fall election (first Tuesday after the first Monday in November) every second even-numbered year, as 1928, 1932, and 1936.

Each state is entitled to as many Electors as it has Senators and Representatives in Congress. If a majority of the 

¹When spoken of as a group, the Presidential Electors are called the Electoral College.

voters in any state are Democrats, then the Democratic Electors are elected; if the majority are Republicans, then 

Republican Electors are elected.¹ Of course, the Electors who are elected in each state are bound by party loyalty to vote for the party's Presidential and Vice-Presidential nominees who were nominated at the National Nominating 

Convention the previous summer.

The Electors meet in their state capitols on the first Monday after the second Wednesday in December following the election, and go through the formality of voting for the President and Vice-President. A certified record of their votes in each state is sent to the President of the United States Senate. On the sixth day of January, a joint session of both houses of Congress is held and, in the presence of all the members, the Electoral votes are formally counted. There are now 531 Electoral votes, because there is one for each 

United States Senator and each Federal Representative.

If no Presidential nominee has a majority of all the Electoral votes, it is the duty of the House of Representatives to choose a President from among the three candidates who had the greatest number of Electoral votes. When this is necessary, the House votes by states, each state having one vote. If no Vice-Presidential nominee receives a majority of all the Electoral votes, it is the duty of the United States Senate to choose one of the two candidates who received the greatest number of Electoral votes.

Even though the President and Vice-President are not formally elected until the early part of January, the nation knows who its next leaders are to be shortly after the polls close on election day in November, because it is soon known whether the Electors from each state will be Dem- 

1The actual votes cast by the voters for Electors are called popular votes; votes cast by Electors in the Electoral College are called Electoral votes. In the 1936 Presidential election Mr. Roosevelt received 523 Electoral votes, and Mr. Landon only 8. Mr. Roosevelt received 27,752,309 popular votes, and Mr. Landon 16,682,524.

Even though Mr. Landon received many popular votes, Republican electors were elected in only two states, neither of which has a large number of Electors.

215 ocratic, Republican, or of some other party. The least number of Electoral votes a nominee can have to be elected is 266, or one more than one-half of all the Electoral votes.

Before the TWENTIETH AMENDMENT was ratified, the 

President and Vice-President were inaugurated on March 

4th following their election. From now on, they will be inaugurated in January following their election.

The Powers of the President. The Constitution is very brief in enumerating the powers and duties of the PRESI- DENT, but they are stated in such a way as to make them broad and inclusive. (Study carefully ARTICLE II, SECTIONS 

2 and 3.)

In brief, the President's powers and duties may be summarized under the following heads: 

(1) Executive and Law-Enforcing Powers. The President is the Chief Executive of the Nation. As such, he is charged with the responsibility of faithfully executing the duties of his office. By his oath he swears to preserve, protect, and defend the Constitution of the United States. This, of course, he must do largely through those whom he appoints to SUBORDINATE OFFICES. 

(2) The Power of Appointment. (Re-read the discussion on APPOINTMENTS beginning on page 87.) One of the greatest powers of the President is that of making appointments of subordinate officials to help him carry out the letter and spirit of the Constitution. ". . . He shall nominate, and by and with the advice and consent of the Senate, shall appoint ambassadors, other public ministers and consuls, judges of the Supreme Court, and all other officers of the United States, whose appointments are not herein otherwise provided for, and which shall be established by law. . ." This great power of appointment causes every President to be besieged by office-seekers who claim to have helped him in his election. To follow a true course in making the thousands of appointments in the interest of good government is no easy responsibility for any President.

The terms of Presidential appointees vary, but most of them are for the term of the President. Judges, and those who gain appointments through Civil Service Examinations are, of course, for indefinite terms. 

(3) Power to Make Treaties. The President has the power to make treaties with foreign nations. Such treaties must be approved, however, by a two-thirds vote of the Senate. 

This power to negotiate treaties is important because approved treaties are binding upon the Nation and must be fulfilled. 

Copyright by Harris and Ewing

THE PRESIDENT ADDRESSING A JOINT SESSION OF CONGRESS IN THE HOUSE OF REPRESENTATIVES 

(See also page 202.) 

(4) Legislative Powers. The President is an executive officer, but he has three important legislative powers: First.

He may call Congress into special session whenever he thinks such a session is necessary and ask them to enact certain legislation. 

Second. "He shall from time to time give to the Congress information of the state of the Union, and recommend to

Federal Government 

217 their consideration such measures as he shall judge necessary and expedient. . ." This he usually does by his annual message which he may deliver in person to a joint meeting of both houses at the opening of each session. 

Third. He has the power to veto acts of Congress, which he disapproves. You have seen, however, that Congress can, by a two-thirds vote in both houses, over-ride the President's veto. 99 

(5) Military and Naval Powers. "The President shall be the commander-in-chief of the army and navy of the United States, and of the militia of the several States, when called into actual service of the United States. . When necessary, the President may use any part of the army or navy to protect the United States or to enforce its laws. The 

President has two advisers (Cabinet members) the Secretary of War and the Secretary of Navy, who actually manage these two great Departments of our Federal government. Then, too, in time of war, some military and naval experts are put at the head of the army and navy. 

(6) Judicial and Pardoning Powers. The President has no actual JUDICIAL POWER, but he can assert his influence in the making of judicial appointments. He appoints the judges (Justices) to the United States Supreme Court and to all the inferior (lower) Federal Courts. 66 ... He shall have power to grant reprieves and pardons for offences against the United States, except in cases of impeachment." A reprieve is a temporary delay of a sentence which a court might impose on a criminal. To pardon a criminal is to set him entirely free. The pardoning power of the President in Federal cases is similar to those of the governors of the various states. 

The President's Cabinet. The President has a group of official advisers known as the Cabinet, sometimes called his 

Official Family. There is no direct provision in the Constitution for such a body, but ARTICLE II, SECTION 2, states 

-"He [the President] may require the opinion, in writing, of the principal officer, in each of the executive departments,

upon any subject relating to the duties of their respective offices..." On the authority of this statement, WASHING- 

TON appointed his first Cabinet of four members, namely, Secretary of State, Secretary of Treasury, Secretary of

War, and Attorney-General. Since WASHINGTON'S time six more members have been added to the President's Cabinet in the following order: Secretary of Navy (1798); Post-

Secretary of Agriculture (1889); Secretary of Commerce and Labor (1903).

The last two offices were separated in 1913. 

Each Cabinet member heads a Department which has general charge of administering that part of the Federal government which comes under its jurisdiction. Each 

DEPARTMENT is divided into separate Divisions, Bureaus, 

'The Post-Office Department was organized by Washington, but its head was not a Cabinet member until 1829. The Attorney-General was a member of Washington's Cabinet, but the Department of Justice was not officially created until 1870.

219 and Commissions;

each with its Chief, Superintendent, or 

Head. Each CABINET MEMBER has several Assistants, and under them, work hundreds, and in some cases thousands, of employees. They are to be found not only in the various government buildings in Washington, D. C., but also in 

Federal offices throughout the entire United States and its territories, and in foreign countries. The greater part of the work of enforcing Federal laws and of carrying out their provisions, is done under the supervision of these ten Executive Departments headed by the members of the President's Cabinet.

CABINET MEMBERS are appointed by the President with the consent of the Senate. Their term is indefinite, but is generally understood to be the same as the President's, as each President has the privilege of choosing his own Cabinet. A Cabinet member's salary is $15,000 per year. The 

President usually holds general Cabinet meetings once or twice a week to discuss the general problems of the Nation. 

He, of course, confers with individual members whenever he chooses. In making final decisions, however, Cabinet members are always subject to the will of the President. 

The Secretary of State. The Secretary of State has charge of the nation's foreign affairs.

He directs the AMBASSA- 

DORS, MINISTERS, and CONSULS who represent the United 

States in foreign countries, and deals with those who represent foreign countries in the United States. He assists in making treaties, and issues the President's proclamations. 

He keeps all Federal records, such as treaties and laws, and has charge of the Great Seal of the United States.

The Secretary of the Treasury. The Secretary of the 

Treasury has charge of the money of the United States. 

Under him are several Assistants, each with definite work. 

There is the Director of the Mint who supervises the coining of all United States gold and silver money and medals. The Superintendent of the Bureau of Engraving and 

Printing has control of the printing of all paper money, bonds, and postage stamps. The TREASURY DEPARTMENT

also has charge of the National banks, and of the collecting of internal revenues. The Secret Service Bureau, the Coast 

Guard Bureau, and the United States Department of Public 

Health all come under the jurisdiction of the TREASURY DEPARTMENT. 

War Department CADETS DRILLING AT WEST POINT. (See page 151.) The Secretary of War. The Secretary of War has charge of the army of the United States. His Department is assisted by a General Staff of expert military men. The WAR 

DEPARTMENT has general charge of the Military Academy at West Point, New York, and the War College in Wash ington, D. C. Coast defense and fortifications, the improvement of rivers and harbors, and the supervision of the governments of Porto Rico and of the Panama Canal Zone all come under the jurisdiction of the WAR DEPARTMENT. 

The Secretary of the Navy. The Secretary of the Navy has control of the navy of the United States. He is assisted by an expert Naval Board. This Department also controls

The Secretary of the Interior. The Secretary of the In terior heads the Department of the Interior which has charge of the important internal affairs not under the control of some other Department. This Department is divided into several Bureaus, each with a definite function. The 

General Land Office has charge of all the land belonging to the United States. The Reclamation Bureau seeks to make waste areas productive and to reclaim waste areas by irrigating dry lands and by draining swamps. The Bureau of 

Indian Affairs has charge of all Indians living on reservations, and controls Indian schools. The Office of Education collects and distributes valuable educational statistics in an effort to benefit the administration of schools.

The Secretary of Labor. The Secretary of Labor and his 

Department seek to improve the condition of laborers the wage-earners. This DEPARTMENT includes the Bureau of Labor Statistics, which gathers and distributes figures on labor conditions throughout the country; the Immigration 

Bureau, which controls the immigration of foreigners; the 

Naturalization Bureau, which has charge of the naturalization of foreigners; and the Women's and Children's Bureaus, which promote the welfare of wage-earning women and the betterment of child life.

4. THE FEDERAL JUDICIAL DEPARTMENT Kinds of Federal Courts. "The judicial power of the 

United States shall be vested in one supreme court, and in such inferior courts as the Congress may from time to time ordain and establish..." (ARTICLE III, SECTION 1, CLAUSE 

1.) Upon the authority of this Constitutional provision,

Congress has established three kinds of Regular Federal 

Courts and four kinds of Special Federal Courts. The REGU- LAR COURTS are: One United States Supreme Court¹; ten Federal Circuit Courts of Appeals; and eighty-five Federal District Courts. The SPECIAL COURTS are: One Court of 

Claims; one Customs Court; one Court of Customs and

Patent Appeals; and Courts for the District of Columbia and for the territories.

The Jurisdiction of Federal Courts. Most of the crimes committed and most of the disputes arising in the every day life of citizens are violations of state laws or are disputes over affairs regulated by state laws. These are, therefore, cases to be tried in some state court. The jurisdiction of 

Federal Courts is solely over those cases arising over disputes on the Federal Constitution, and our crimes against or conflicts with Federal laws.

The Constitution clearly states the jurisdiction of the 

FEDERAL COURTS in ARTICLE III, SECTION 2, CLAUSES 1 and 

2. These Clauses should be thoroughly studied; you will see that Federal Courts pass on cases which affect the 

United States as a whole.

The United States Supreme Court. The United States 

Supreme Court is the highest court in the land. It is composed of one Chief Justice and eight Associate Justices. Its chief duty is to interpret the Constitution when any charge is made that it has been violated by Congress (through the enactment of laws), by a state legislature, or by any other body or individual. The decisions of the SUPREME COURT 

'Do not confuse the United States Supreme Court with the Supreme Courts of the various states; nor any other Federal Courts with state courts, even though their names are similar.

THE SUPREME COURT OF THE UNITED STATES- OF JUSTICE IN THE LAND THE HIGHEST COURT 

(A picture of the building in which this Court sits is on the front cover of this book.) 

are final. It has both original and appellate jurisdiction; that is, it may hear certain cases that may be tried directly before the SUPREME COURT (original cases) or cases that are being appealed from a lower court, where one party was dissatisfied with the lower court's decision (an appel late case). The SUPREME COURT sits in Washington, D. C. 

Federal Circuit Courts of Appeals. Federal Circuit Courts of Appeals were established by Congress in 1891 to relieve the work of the United States Supreme Court. There are 

TEN such COURTS, and each serves a definite district or region. 

Each of these CIRCUIT COURTS OF APPEALS is composed of from three to six judges. They hear only cases which are appealed from lower Courts.

Federal District Courts. In rank, Federal District Courts are the lowest of the regular Federal Courts. The forty-

Federal Government 

239 eight states are divided into eighty-five districts, each state forming one or more districts. In some districts there is just one judge, in others more than one. There are one hundred forty-five judges in the eighty-five districts. These courts have jurisdiction over all the ordinary violations of 

Federal laws. 10 5 3 

MAP SHOWING THE DISTRICTS OF THE FEDERAL CIRCUIT COURTS OF APPEALS The Special Federal Courts: The Court of Claims consists of five judges who hear cases of money claims against the United States Government. It sits in Washington, D. C. 

The Court of Customs consists of nine judges who hear cases of dispute over tariff laws. This COURT sits at the principal seaports where most of such cases arise. 

The Court of Customs and Patent Appeals consists of five judges who hear cases on customs which are appealed from the COURT OF CUSTOMS, and cases regarding disputes over patents. This Court sits in Washington, D. C. 

The COURTS for the District of Columbia and our outlying territories are Federal Courts and hear such cases as come under their special jurisdiction in those places.

United States District Attorneys and United States Mar shals. United States District Attorneys and United States

ATTORNEY'S duty is similar to that of a county attorney,

or of a state district attorney. That is, to bring suit against offenders of Federal laws.

The duty of UNITED STATES MARSHAL is to arrest offend ers and take them to Court at the proper time. He has custody of prisoners and carries out the wishes of the Court regarding the offender. He is an officer of the Federal Court.

UNIT V. THE GOVERNMENT OF THE DIS- TRICT OF COLUMBIA AND OUR OUTLYING POSSESSIONS THE PROBLEMS In PART TWO, UNIT I, of this book you read about the NEED FOR the various DIVISIONS or UNITS of our government. 

You learned that the Federal government is a government for the entire nation, and as such, must have a seat or capital. When our Federal government was begun, all the land suitable for a capital site was part of some state. 

Hence, the Federal government had to secure land from some state on which to build its capital city. It secured the land (THE DISTRICT OF COLUMBIA) and built the city (WASHINGTON). The DISTRICT needed a government, as it was not a part of any state. It is the purpose of part of this UNIT to help you learn how the Federal government secured a capital site, built a capital city, and gave it a form of government. 

Our nation was not old before it began to acquire land beyond the bounds of what can be called the United States proper-beyond what is now the forty-eight states. This 

UNIT will also guide you into the study of how we acquired those lands (OUR OUTLYING POSSESSIONS) and how they are governed. It will help you answer such questions as:

(1) How is our CAPITAL CITY (WASHINGTON) governed? 

(2) How did we acquire our OUTLYING POSSESSIONS? and (3) How are our OUTLYING POSSESSIONS governed today? 

1. THE GOVERNMENT OF THE DISTRICT OF COLUMBIA Origin of the District of Columbia. Our Capital City- 

Washington. There was no central (or Federal) government for the American colonies before the movement to revolt from England began. The first gathering of repre- 243

sentatives from the various colonies to discuss relations with England met in Philadelphia in September of 1774. 

From then on, our Federal government led a wandering life until in the fall of 1800, it settled in its permanent home at 

WASHINGTON. The Continental Congress and the Congress under the Articles of the Confederation held sessions in eight different cities in four states.¹ 

HE BE From an Old Steel Engraving AN EARLY PICTURE OF THE FEDERAL CAPITOL AT WASHINGTON 

(See also picture on page 197.)

The first Congress under the Constitution of the United States was called to meet in New York City, March 4, 1789, but it did not get fully assembled until early April of that year. It moved to Philadelphia, Dec. 6, 1790, where it stayed for ten years. During the summer and fall of 1800, the entire Federal government moved to Washington, D. C. 

¹Congress began sessions as follows: Philadelphia, Pa., Sept. 5, 1774; Baltimore, Md., Dec. 12, 1776; Philadelphia, Pa., Feb. 27, 1777; 

Lancaster, Pa., Sept. 27, 1777; York, Pa., Sept. 30, 1777; Philadelphia, Pa., June 27, 1778; Princeton, N. J., June 30, 1783; Annapolis, Md., Nov. 26, 1783; Trenton, N. J., Nov. 1, 1784; New York City, N. Y., Jan. 11, 1785. Some of the changes during the Revolutionary

War were made for military reasons. Someone has said our Federal government was like a "poor relation" without a home.

The District of Columbia 

245 Discussions began in Congress about a site for a permanent home for the Federal government during Revolutionary days. Four states, New York, New Jersey, Maryland, and Virginia, made offers to Congress for a capital site. 

Nothing definite was done about the matter until after the new Constitution of the United States had been adopted. 

ARTICLE I, SECTION 8, CLAUSE 17, gave Congress power to provide a Federal District and create a capital city. The 

Clause reads: "To exercise exclusive legislation in all cases 591 

From an Old Steel Engraving AN EARLY DAY PICTURE OF THE WHITE HOUSE, WASHINGTON 

(See also picture on page 211.) whatsoever over such district (not exceeding ten miles square) as may, by cession of particular states, and the acceptance of Congress, become the seat of the government of the United States. ..."

There was much discussion and debating, sometimes bitter, over the location of the site. Both the northern states and the southern states wanted the honor of having the Federal capital. A site was finally selected on the Potomac 

River between the Little Falls and Alexandria, Virginia.

The original tract contained land on both sides of the River, in Virginia and Maryland.

As soon as the location of the site was settled, PRESIDENT 

WASHINGTON and a COMMISSION OF THREE were given au thority to lay out plans and begin the Federal city.¹

It was decided that the city should be built on the north side of the Potomac, but it was not until 1846 that the land in the DISTRICT south of the river was returned to the state of Virginia. The DISTRICT now contains about seventy square miles of land and water area. For a while, the DIS- 

TRICT was called the Territory of Columbia, but later Congress officially named it the District of Columbia. The city was spoken of as WASHINGTON as early as 1791, but was not officially named Washington by Congress until 1796. 

PRESIDENT WASHINGTON and his Commission secured the services of MAJOR PIERRE CHARLES L'ENFANT to lay out the plans for the city. MAJOR L'ENFANT was a French army engineer, who had come here to help the Colonial cause dur ing the Revolutionary War. Both PRESIDENT WASHINGTON 

and MAJOR L'ENFANT deserve much credit for their efforts and foresight in planning a capital city that can still be a worthy seat of government for a nation whose area has expanded to the Pacific coast, and whose population has increased to over 125,000,000 people. 

The city of Washington, fortunately, has been kept a capital city—a residential city. Factories and business concerns which would mar its beauty have not been encouraged to locate in its midst. The city, with its broad, clean streets and avenues, its beautiful trees and parks, its stately government buildings, its many, significant monuments and memorials, its interesting foreign legations and embassies, its quiet location on the broad Potomac crowned in the center with our magnificently-domed capitol, has often been proclaimed to be the most beautiful capital city in the world. 

The Commission appointed by Washington consisted of Thomas 

Johnson and Daniel Carroll of Maryland, and David Stuart of Virginia.

The District of Columbia 

247 GEORGE WASHINGTON himself never lived in the city he worked so hard to create and which is named for him. He died in 1799. PRESIDENT JOHN ADAMS became the first master of the partially finished White House, when the government moved from Philadelphia in the summer and fall of 1800. 

The Government of Our Capital City. The government of the city of Washington is unique in that it is not selfruled like any other American city. Instead, it is governed by the Federal Congress. There are no county or state laws by which it must abide. It is not a part of any county or state; but a separate municipality subject only to the government of the United States. 

Since 1878, when the Commission form of government was established for the DISTRICT, the residents lost all voting privileges. They have no voice in the DISTRICT'S government, neither are they represented in Congress. They do not even vote for the President of the United States. 

Federal government officials who are temporarily residing in Washington, however, may go to their home states for elections and vote.

The city of Washington got its first charter from Congress in 1802, which provided for a mayor to be appointed by the President and a council elected by the people. Slight changes in this form of government were made in 1812 and 1820. Up to 1871, there were other municipalities within the DISTRICT besides the city of Washington. But in 1871, Congress abolished the other municipal governments within the District and established one government for the District of Columbia. The new government for the entire District consisted of executive officers and boards appointed by the President; a house of delegates; and an elected delegate to the national House of Representatives. This was changed in 

1874, when Congress created a government for the District headed by COMMISSIONERS. This was made the permanent form of government in 1878. With minor changes in details, this last form is the one in operation at present.

Baltimore and Ohio Railroad

THE CLASSIC LINCOLN MEMORIAL AT WASHINGTON, D. C. 

The government is in the hands of three Commissioners appointed by the President and approved by the Senate. 

Two of them must be regular residents of the District and the third must be from the engineering corps of the United States army. These three Commissioners are the chief executive officers of the District. The Congress is the legislature for the District; but it has, by various acts, empowered the Commissioners to make rules and regulations. 

The judges for the COURTS are appointed by the President. 

There are a court of appeals, a supreme court, a municipal court, a police court, a probate court, and a juvenile court. 

The President, the Commissioners, and the supreme court of the District appoint all the administrative boards and officers necessary to carry on regular municipal government for the District.

Like any other large municipality, the District has many BOARDS and DEPARTMENTS, such as a fire and police depart-

The District of Columbia 

249 ment, a public works department, a health department, a board of education, a park board, and a board of charities. 

They in turn carry out the duties they are expected to perform, like similar departments in any municipality. 

The expenses necessary to maintain the District's government are paid partly by the Federal government and partly by taxes, licenses, fees, and fines collected from the residents of the District.

 1. SHOW ON AN OUTLINE MAP the size of the United States when its capital site was chosen. Shade that section east of the Appalachian Highlands which contained nearly all of the population of the country. Locate the capital. Was it centrally located at that time? Could our capital be better protected today if it were located nearer the center of the 

United States? Why?

4. DISCUSS: Do you think GEORGE WASHINGTON and MAJOR 

L'ENFANT deserve much credit for their foresight in planning a city that could expand in size as the Nation expanded in size? 

5. CLASS REPORT: MAJOR L'ENFANT was dismissed by PRESIDENT 

WASHINGTON before the city was well under way because he did not agree with the Commissioners on some details. He died in 1825, a disappointed man, and was buried in a private cemetery in an unmarked grave. Recently his body was reburied among our other national heroes in beautiful Arlington cemetery across the river from the city he planned. A fitting monument has been placed over it. Try to find more about 

L'Enfant's life and work in your library and report to your class. 

6. CLASS PROJECT: The following numbers of the National 

Geographic Magazine contain beautiful pictures of our capital city, its buildings, monuments, and beauty spots. Below many of the pictures are interesting comments from which you will learn much. Secure as many as possible, and display

them in your classroom or give floor talks about them: June, 1913; March, 1915; August, 1922; June, 1923; March, 1930; and November, 1931. 

7. DISCUSS: Would you like to be a resident of the city of 

Washington when you reach voting age and not be allowed to vote or have a voice in your municipal or Federal government? 

2. HOW WE HAVE ACQUIRED OUR OUTLYING POSSESSIONS The United States Proper. The United States was born by the Peace of Paris, 1783, which formally ended the Revolutionary War. It was at that time, roughly speaking, all the land east of the Mississippi river, south of the Great Lakes and the St. Lawrence river, and north of Florida. 

From 1783 to 1853 the United States gradually expanded. 

With the Gadsden Purchase in 1853, it reached its present size. (See previous discussion of the GROWTH OF THE 

UNITED STATES on page 22 and map on page 171.)

Alaska, Our First Outlying Possession. From 1853 until after the Civil War, the United States acquired no more territory. Shortly after the Civil War, however, Russia offered to sell us her colony of Alaska. The Russian government had been friendly to the Union during the War, so 

SECRETARY OF STATE WILLIAM H. SEWARD purchased it in 

1867 for $7,200,000 or about two cents an acre. At first the purchase was frowned upon as being "unnecessary" and a "very poor bargain." It was spoken of as "Seward's icebox" and an "Arctic waste." It soon turned out to be a better bargain than anyone thought it would be at the time of its purchase. Its principal resources, fish (largely salmon), copper, gold, and furs have brought the United

States its purchase price many times over. It has an area of about 586,400 square miles. In 1930 it had a population of approximately 59,200 people, about one-half of which were Eskimos. Its principal cities are JUNEAU (its capital), KETCHIKAN, ANCHORAGE, FAIRBANKS, and NOME.

IN MEMOR KAUKISH DIED ACE BY Canadian Pacific Railway KIK-SETTI TOTEM, ALASKA 

Totem poles such as these have a deep religious significance to the 

Alaska Eskimo. (Other Alaskan pictures, pages 252 and 268.)

The United States Becomes a World Power Around 1900. 

Up until the 1900 period, the United States could not be called a World Power. It had no possessions in other parts of the world except ALASKA. But during the few years before and after 1900, many thousands of square miles of territory (mostly islands) were added to the United States as outlying possessions. 

Canadian Pacific Railway A VIEW OF KETCHIKAN, ALASKA 

(See Map, page 267. Other Alaskan pictures, pages 251 and 268.)

These possessions were acquired at different times, for different purposes, and by different methods. By acquiring them in different parts of the world, protecting and improving them, and reaping benefits from them, the United States has truly become a WORLD POWER. 

American Samoa. The native Samoans gave the United 

States a harbor for a coaling station in 1878. Germany and

GUAM MAP SHOWING OUTLYING POSSESSIONS AND PROTECTORATES OF THE UNITED STATES 

Great Britain also acquired rights in these islands, and they gradually came under the control of the three nations. 

This joint control, however, did not work out very satisfactorily. On one occasion, in 1889, the countries almost came to open warfare. PRESIDENT CLEVELAND urged that the Islands be divided among the three nations. This plan was carried out in part in 1899, and the United States took the island of Tutuila, which is commonly called American Samoa. The island has an area of 76 square miles, and in 

1930 had a population of about 10,000 people. Its largest community, PAGO PAGO, has a good harbor and has become an important coaling station for us in the South Seas. 

The Philippines, Porto Rico, and Guam. The large West 

Indian island of Cuba, often called the "Pearl of the Antilles," had always belonged to Spain. Previous to the last decade of the nineteenth century, the sugar and tobacco

industry had prospered on the island. American business men became interested in these industries and had invested large amounts of money in them. During this same period the Cubans were attempting to get their independence from Spain because of the harsh and sometimes cruel treatment they were receiving at the hands of Spanish officers. 

FORT SANTIAGO, PHILIPPINE ISLANDS War Department 

The United States began to look with displeasure upon the cruel treatment of our friendly Cuban neighbors. 

The situation came to a climax on the night of February 15, 1898. One of our finest battleships, the Maine, which was on a friendly mission to Havan (Cuba), was sunk by an explosion of some kind in the Havana harbor. Some 260 men were killed, and the ship destroyed. This, of course, led to a declaration of war on Spain. 

The war was a short one. Most of the fighting took place

in the Philippine Islands and Cuba and in the waters surrounding them. The peace treaty was signed in Paris on 

December 10, 1898. Spain was forced to withdraw from 

Cuba and give up all claim to the island-a claim which she had held for over four hundred years! The United

States did not take Cuba, but allowed it to become a Republic under her guidance and protection. It is still a protectorate of the United States, and the United States stands ready as its protector.

The United States, however, gained three possessions as a result of the war. Spain was forced to sell us the Philippine Islands for $20,000,000, and to cede to us the islands of Porto Rico and Guam. (See map, page 253.)

The Philippine Islands, an archipelago of over 3,000 individual islands, many of which are small and uninhabited, have a total area of 114,400 square miles and a population of approximately 12,600,000 people.¹ The principal cities are MANILA (the capital) on the island of Luzon; ILOILO (Elo-Elo) on the island of Panay (Pa-nī); and ZEBU on the island of Zebu.

Porto Rico has an area of 3,435 square miles and a population of approximately 1,500,000 people. Its principal cities are its capital, SAN JUAN (San Wan), MAYAGUEZ (Mä'yä-gwäs) and PONCE (Pönsā). (Map, page 275.) 

Guam (Gwäm) has an area of 206 square miles and an approximate population of 18,500 people. Its chief city and capital is AGANA (Ä-gän'-yä). (Map, page 279.) 

The Spanish-American War of 1898, therefore, started the United States on the road to true world power. 

The Hawaiian Islands. CAPTAIN Cook discovered these mid-Pacific Islands in 1778 and named them the Sandwich 

Islands in honor of the English EARL OF SANDWICH.2 How ever, the native name Hawaii (Hä-wi-e) or Hawaiian 

Islands is more commonly used now than Sandwich Islands. 'See later discussion on Philippine Independence, page 272 ff. "The Earl of Sandwich had a fondness for eating cold meat between two slices of bread while he played cards, hence the word "sandwich."

American missionaries began going to the Islands as early as 1820. They took American customs and ideals with them. Many American settlers followed the missionaries during the next half century. (Map, page 271.) 

The ideal climate of the Islands made them popular, and 

American capitalists soon found ways of investing money in resorts and plantations there. The American population grew rapidly and began to get control of the industries and the government. (See pictures, pages 263, 270.)

As early as 1893 there was an attempt to annex the 

Islands to the United States, but PRESIDENT CLEVELAND and the Hawaiian queen (Queen Liliuokalani) both opposed an nexation. However, in 1898, PRESIDENT MCKINLEY ap proved the annexation, and the dethroned queen was given a pension by the United States. She lived until 1917. The annexation of the Hawaiian Islands added 6,449 square miles of territory to our outlying possessions. They have an approximate population of 368,300 people which includes, besides Americans and natives, many Japanese, Chinese, and Filipinos. The principal cities are HONOLULU 

(the capital) on the island of Oahu (Ō-ä'hoo); and HILO (He-lō) on the island of Hawaii. 

The Panama Canal Zone. In 1878, a French Company, which had built the Suez Canal, secured the right from Colombia to dig a canal across the isthmus of Panama. (The isthmus, at that time, belonged to Colombia.) This French 

Panama Canal Company began work on the isthmus in 1881, but its funds were mismanaged and the loss of life of workmen from tropical diseases was terrific. After it had spent nearly a quarter of a billion dollars on the project, the Company gave up its efforts and collapsed in 1889. 

The United States government was aroused to see a need of a canal through the isthmus when the Oregon, one of our best battleships which happened to be on the Pacific coast, was compelled to go around Cape Horn in order to get to Cuba during the SPANISH-AMERICAN WAR. Furthermore, our government realized that a short-cut from the Atlantic

to the Pacific would be helpful, now that we owned valuable possessions in the Pacific.

PRESIDENT THEODORE ROOSEVELT and Congress started negotiations soon after he (Roosevelt) became President. 

Two parties had to be dealt with: Colombia for the right to go through the isthmus, and the French Company for the right to go ahead with what had already been done. 

The French Company's rights and materials were bought in 1902 for $40,000,000. Colombia, however, demanded a much higher sum for a narrow strip of land we wanted, than President Roosevelt and Congress were willing to pay. 

It looked, for a while, as though there would be no Panama Canal.

The people of Panama, however, were eager to have the United States build a canal through their land, as they knew they would benefit by it. Accordingly, they revolted from their mother country, Colombia, in 1903, and established for themselves a new republic, the Republic of 

Panama. 

Almost immediately (1904) the United States made a treaty with the new Republic whereby we bought the Canal Zone for a down payment of $10,000,000 and an annual payment, beginning in 1913, of $250,000. The treaty also reserved the right of the United States to protect the entire new Republic of Panama if necessary.

The Canal Zone is a strip of land about 10 miles wide and approximately 50 miles long. Its area is approximately 550 square miles and its population 

39,450, which includes about 7,000 Americans.

MAP SHOWING THE REPUBLIC OF PANAMA, the Panama Canal Zone, and the route of the Canal.

The principal cities are COLON and CRISTOBAL on the Atlantic end of the Zone; and BALBOA on the Pacific end. 

The city of PANAMA, which is the capital of the Republic

of Panama, near the Pacific end of the canal, is not within the Canal Zone; but the United States has the right to enforce sanitary ordinances and to maintain public order in it if she finds it necessary to do so. 

The Virgin Islands.¹ A group of about fifty small islands just east of Porto Rico in the north eastern end of the Caribbean Sea belonged to Denmark and were commonly called the Danish West Indies. During the World War our government thought it wise to get control of as much of the doorway to the Caribbean as possible, so as to be better able to protect the Panama Canal, which had been opened for use shortly before the War broke out. Accordingly, in 1917 we bought this group of islands, now called the 

Virgin Islands, from Denmark for $25,000,000. 

The three principal islands of the group are: St. Croix,

St. Thomas, and St. John. The area of the entire group is about 133 square miles. The population, which is chiefly negro, numbers about 22,000. The principal cities are ST. 

THOMAS on the island of St. Thomas; and CHRISTIANSTED and FREDERKSTED, both on the island of St. Croix (Croy).

Other Insular2 Possessions and Protectorates. The United 

States owns a few other scattered, small islands most of which are unimportant and uninhabited, hence, need no government. Four of these, Wake, Midway, Howland, and

Baker Islands are scattered in the mid-Pacific; and the 

Guano Islands are along the Peruvian coast of South 

America. 

Besides owning the various outlying possessions just discussed, the United States acts as a sort of "big brother" to five so-called independent Republics: Cuba, Panama, Santo

Domingo, Haiti, and Nicaragua. Each of these has in some way had difficulty in establishing or maintaining its selfgovernment, and the United States has stepped in to prevent other nations from doing so. In other words, the 

United States has attempted to uphold the Monroe Doctrine. 

¹See map, page 278. 

2 Island.

STREET SCENE IN HAVANA, CUBA, SHOWING THE CAPITOL IN THE BACKGROUND 

(See question 3, page 264.) These countries are commonly referred to as our protectorates, as we protect them when they need protection.

We have already seen how CUBA and the REPUBLIC OF 

PANAMA became our protectorates. (See pages 255 and 256 ff.) 

The Republic of Santo Domingo (or the Dominican Re public, as it is sometimes called) on the eastern end of the island of Haiti became bankrupt soon after 1900. Some of its European creditors (France, Italy, and Belgium) asked

ALCAZAR (PALACE) OF DIEGO COLUMBUS, SANTO DOMINGO 

the United States to step in and manage its financial affairs.

In 1905, PRESIDENT THEODORE ROOSEVELT did so. With the consent of its President, ROOSEVELT sent to Santo Domingo, officials to make a check-up on its condition and to manage its future financial affairs. The little Republic is still independent, but it is so under the watchful eyes of the 

United States. 

In the years just previous to 1914, SANTO DOMINGO'S neighbor, the Republic of Haiti, which occupies the western end of the island of Haiti, had considerable trouble in maintaining a stable government. During the year of 1914 and the early part of 1915, the little Republic found itself troubled with financial and political problems. Some of these were so serious that they led to riots and even massacres. The United States, of course, did not like to see such conditions continue in a place so near SANTO DOMINGO. 

Then, too, Americans owned considerable property in Haiti which the Federal government wanted to protect. Furthermore, the United States deemed it wise to enforce the provisions of the MONROE DOCTRINE, so in July of 1915, officers and marines were sent there to take charge of the little

RUINS OF AN OLD FORT IN HAITI 

country. As a result of this occupation, a treaty was made which gave the United States complete control of Haiti's financial affairs and rather general supervision over its entire government. In recent years, the HAITIANS have been gradually regaining some self-control of their government. 

It is still an independent Republic, but like SANTO DO- 

MINGO, the United States stands by like a big brother.

Nicaragua, somewhat like SANTO DOMINGO and HAITI, has had a more or less stormy political and financial history since it gained its independence from Spain in 1821. The 

United States, however, did not step in until 1909, after there had been a rather serious revolution in NICARAGUA.

Shortly after this revolution, New York bankers loaned the Nicaraguan government money. From then on the United 

States took a "protective" attitude toward American inter-

ests in the country, and marines were kept there intermittently until 1932. The United States Senate has refused to make the country a legal protectorate of ours, but in 1916 a treaty was made which contains two important provisions: One gives the United States the exclusive right to build and maintain an ocean-to-ocean canal through Nicaragua. The other gives us a 99-year lease on certain coastal territory on which to build a naval base. 

How the United States Has Helped Its Outlying Posses sions. A glance at a map of the world tells us that all of our principal outlying possessions and protectorates, except Alaska, are closer to the equator than even the southernmost parts of the United States proper. Most of them are also surrounded by warm waters and have heavy rainfalls. This gives them tropical climates. Before the beginning of the twentieth century, the people of these regions, for the most part, were largely natives. As a rule, they were not familiar with the methods of living and the comforts of life that most Americans were enjoying.

Since they came under the control of the United States, these natives have felt American influence. Orderly governments have been established, many of which have been modeled after that of the United States. EDUCATION has been encouraged, and schools have been built and maintained. Thousands of American teachers have gone to 

Alaska, Hawaii, the Philippines, Cuba, and Porto Rico.

With them have gone American methods and ideals. Many of the unsanitary conditions caused by the tropical climate and ignorance have been remedied by sanitary engineers, doctors, teachers, and nurses. Diseases have been controlled, and the death rates decreased. Modern agricultural knowledge and methods have been introduced to take the place of the crude and ancient methods used by the natives. 

Improved methods of manufacturing and transportation have increased their trade and commerce. Roads and bridges have been constructed. Electricity in its various forms has worked its wonders there as elsewhere. Even

those who criticize the United States for extending her control to these places must admit that along with American control have gone improved conditions of living. And in most cases, without a doubt, the natives are happier.

3. THE GOVERNMENT OF OUR LARGER POSSESSIONS The Relationship of Our Federal Government to That of 

Our Possessions. The nineteenth century had not advanced far before our FEDERAL GOVERNMENT began to be recognized as the supreme authority of our Nation. As such, it established a government for each new region that was acquired between the Mississippi River and the Pacific Ocean.

The general practice was to organize a newly-settled region into a territory. When the Territory had sufficient inhabitants, had built up a working government, and had framed

Government of Our Larger Possessions 265 

a satisfactory constitution, the Federal government would admit it into the Union as a State with its own self-government. It then fully participated in the government of the 

United States-it became a part of the whole. (See previ ous discussion on How NEW STATES ORIGINATED, beginning on page 172.)

When the United States began to acquire lands beyond the bounds of North America, however, new problems arose regarding their government. Except Alaska, all of our outlying possessions are in tropical regions. When acquired, they were inhabited largely by natives who were entirely inexperienced in self-government as expressed in our Constitution. The Federal government, therefore, could not admit them to the Union with LOCAL SELF-GOVERNMENTS 

as it did in the case of the Territories in the United States proper. The United States had to provide a government for all its possessions, but not necessarily a government with all the RIGHTS and DEMOCRATIC IDEAS that are expressed in the Constitution. It felt that the inhabitants of most of its tropical possessions were unprepared to manage a form of government such as is outlined by our Constitution. The United States Supreme Court decided in the famous "Insular Cases" that our Constitution does not necessarily have to follow our flag. In other words, the Supreme Court said that the United States may claim and govern lands, but it does not need to give the inhabitants the same rights and privileges that are enjoyed by the people of the United States proper.

Following this procedure, the Federal government has created two general types of government for the OUTLYING 

POSSESSIONS: One, incorporated or organized territorial government; and the other, unincorporated or unorganized territorial government. Only two of our possessions are considered organized territories and as such have an organized territorial form of government. They are Alaska and the Hawaiian Islands. Their governments are very similar to those that were given to the organized territories

of the United States proper before they became states of the Union. They enjoy a great deal of self-government, but are not equal in rank with the states of the Union. 

All other possessions are considered unorganized territories, and as such do not enjoy the rights and privileges of as much self-government as the organized territories. 

The Government of Alaska. Alaska had no self-government until 1912. Previous to that time, all its laws were made by Congress, and were carried out by executive and judicial officers who were appointed by the President. In 

1912, however, largely through the efforts of PRESIDENT TAFT, Congress passed the Civil Government Act for Alaska. This Act made it an organized territory, and as such, gave it a government much like that of any of our continental organized territories before they became fullfledged states.

The chief executive officer is the territorial governor who is appointed for a term of four years by the President and confirmed by the Senate of the United States. With the approval of the President, the GOVERNOR appoints his chief aides and the heads of the various executive departments of the government. Some of the CHIEF DEPARTMENTS and 

BUREAUS are: land office, education, immigration, mine service, health, and fish and game bureaus.

The legislature is composed of a senate and a house of representatives. The SENATE has eight members who serve four years; and the HOUSE has sixteen members who serve two years. The members of both houses are popularly elected by districts. The legislature meets regularly every two years, but the governor may call special sessions for urgent reasons. 

There are four United States District Courts in Alaska.

Each is presided over by a judge who is appointed for a four-year term by the President and confirmed by the 

United States Senate. Minor local courts may be created when necessary, and the United States district judges appoint the officers for them.

Government of Our Larger Possessions 267 

Settlements with 300 or more inhabitants may organize a municipal government for local purposes if they wish, but there are only a few such organized municipalities. Most of the population is content to live under TERRITORIAL CON- 

TROL. 

Alaska sends an elected Territorial Delegate to each of our sessions of Congress. He has a seat in the House of

Representatives. He may serve on Committees and enter into debates but he cannot vote. His principal duty is to make known to Congress the wants and needs of Alaska.

The ALASKAN GOVERNMENT is a self-government, its in habitants are citizens of the United States, yet Congress reserves the right to pass on all its acts, and to approve or disapprove of them as it sees fit. If it disapproves, the act (or law) becomes invalid. Hence, the United States keeps a watchful eye on its northernmost possession.

The Government of the Hawaiian Islands. The govern ment of the Hawaiian Islands is very similar in structure to that of Alaska. Congress made the Islands an organized

Canadian Pacific Railway TAKU GLACIER, ALASKA 

(Other Alaskan pictures, pages 251 and 252.) territory in 1900, soon after we annexed them. All citizens of the Islands were granted American citizenship.

The chief executive is the territorial governor who is appointed by the President with the consent of the Senate of the United States for a four-year term. He must be a citizen of the territory, and at least 35 years of age. The 

GOVERNOR has the usual executive powers of appointment. 

He appoints his chiefs aides who head the various executive departments and bureaus of the government. Among others, there are: the attorney-general, treasurer, secretary, commissioner of public lands, commissioner of agriculture, superintendent of public works.

The legislature is composed of two houses: A senate of fifteen members who serve four-year terms; and a house of representatives of thirty members who serve two-year

The LEGISLATURE meets regularly every other year. It enacts most of the laws which control the people of the Islands, but Congress has, by various restrictions, limited its power in financial matters.

The HAWAIIAN ISLANDS, being an organized territory, enjoy considerable self-government. But, like the government of Alaska, it is closely supervised by its owner and protector, the United States. 

The Philippine Islands. A third large outlying possession of the United States was the Philippine Islands. When the 

Islands were taken by the United States in 1898, they were not ready for any kind of self-government. The people had had no training for it under Spanish rule; and, for the most part, the masses were totally uneducated. Tribes living in both the southern and northern parts of the archipelago were quite uncivilized. It has been impossible to change such a condition rapidly; but it is being done slowly by the government, the schools, and the churches. Shortly after we took the Islands, PRESIDENT MCKINLEY said: "The Phil ippines are not ours to exploit, but to develop, to civilize, to educate, and to train in the science of self-government." 

In 1916, when Congress passed the Jones Act, it stated: "It is, as has always been, the purpose of the people of the United States to withdraw their sovereignty over the Philippine Islands and to recognize their independence as soon as a stable government can be established therein." 

The Former Government of the Phlippines. During the

first few years of American ownership, the government of the Philippines was largely military, and was subject to the orders of the President. In 1901, the President appointed a Commission to govern the Islands, and gradually to expand the government so that the natives could take what part they could in their own control. For several years this 

Commission was headed by the late William Howard Taft, who did a great deal to organize a stable civil government for the Filipino people and get them started on the road to self-government. A Philippine legislature was established in 1907, in which the COMMISSION formed the upper house, the lower house being elected. In 1913, PRESIDENT WILSON increased the self-government by appointing on the Commission a majority of Filipinos. The Jones Act of 1916 gave the Islands a still more liberal government and provided for popular election of a Senate. 

The Filipinos Demand Their Independence. Since 1907, when a law which made it a crime for any Filipino to advocate the independence of the Islands was repealed, there has been constant agitation for such a movement. The 

Filipinos want their independence, and they have repeatedly told the United States so. The government of the United 

States has listened to their demands, but has moved very cautiously and slowly when considering them. 

The Governor-General of the Islands during the years of 

1913 to 1921 was Francis B. Harrison. His policy was to allow as many Filipinos as possible to hold government positions and to give the legislature the maximum amount of freedom. He wanted the Filipinos to govern themselves as much as possible. The government, under his governorship, even took control of some of the industries of the 

Islands. 

Governor-General Harrison was replaced in 1921 by General Leonard Wood, and the policy changed. General Wood believed there had been too much freedom and began to use his veto power on the Philippine legislature. Naturally, the Filipinos resented his acts and agitation for indepen-

Government of Our Larger Possessions 

273 dence became very intense.

Petitions were even presented to PRESIDENT COOLIDGE, asking for the recall of General Wood. PRESIDENT COOLIDGE wanted to get first hand in formation on the problem, so he sent COLONEL CARMI A. 

THOMPSON to the Islands to make a thorough investigation and report the facts to him. The report suggested that selfgovernment be granted the Filipinos as rapidly as possible, but that complete independence be withheld for some time. 

Agitation for complete independence continued, however, and the Hare-Hawes-Cutting Bill was introduced into the Seventy-first Congress. This bill, if passed, would have provided for the gradual freeing of the Islands over a five-year period. It also provided for the protection of 

American property in the Islands, and for permanent United States naval bases. The Seventy-first Congress adjourned, however, on March 4, 1931, before the bill was voted upon.

The HARE-HAWES-CUTTING BILL, with some changes, was again introduced into the Seventy-second Congress and passed over PRESIDENT HOOVER'S veto in January, 1933. 

Among other things, the bill as passed, provided for a 

Philippine Convention to draw up a Constitution within two years; then complete independence after ten more years if their Constitution and government worked satisfactorily. 

It further provided for a limited importation of Philippine sugar, coconut oil, and hemp into the United States during the ten-year period; for full tariff duties on Philippine products after the ten-year period; and for permanent 

United States military- and naval-base rights in the Islands.

The bill was attacked and praised by both American and Philippine political and commercial leaders and was finally rejected by the Filipinos.

The Philippines Secure Their Independence. Agitation for independence grew stronger than ever, and soon a revised plan was in the making. This plan, the Tydings-McDuffie 

Bill, similar to the HARE-HAWES-CUTTING BILL, but with certain objectionable features eliminated, was passed by

Congress and signed by PRESIDENT ROOSEVELT in 1934. This 

Act was unanimously accepted by the Filipinos. 

The Act provided for the framing of a Constitution to form the basis of the new Philippine government. This was done immediately, and the Constitution was approved both by PRESIDENT ROOSEVELT and the Philippine voters in 1935. For a ten-year period the new Philippine Common wealth Government, similar in structure to our own, will be under rather close supervision of the United States. A resident United States High Commissioner will watch the progress of the new government and see that all the provisions of the TYDINGS-MCDUFFIE BILL (The Independence Act) 

are carried out. If the plan fully materializes, the Philippine Islands will be given complete freedom on July 4, 1946. Thus, the United States will have granted independence to one of its largest outlying possessions.
