Until present-day methods of transportation and communication began to develop, one section of the country knew little about what other sections were doing. Adjoining states were strangers. Even communities located twenty miles apart knew little about each other. Life was, indeed, simple and independent; especially when contrasted with present-day conditions.

Present-Day Life Is Complex and Dependent. Today, the life of the average American is far different from that just described. It is more complex and more dependent. Our interests and activities today extend far beyond our homes. 

Our necessities are no longer gotten only from our immediate neighborhood, but from the far corners of the earth. 

Rapid and cheap means of communication and transportation have brought about the change. Fast trains, huge motor trucks, airplanes, and great steamships bring us com-

Greyhound Lines A NEW TYPE BUS. This is a popular form of transportation. 

modities from far away places. We, in turn, send the products of our farms and factories to distant lands. Villages, cities, states, and even nations, separated by great distances, are no longer strangers. The telephone, telegraph, efficient postal systems, and radio; the automobile, good roads, fast trains, steamers, and the airplane have made them neighbors. And they are neighbors who depend upon each other for the things each other produces. School systems have taught people to read, so newspapers, magazines, and libraries can bring a knowledge of the outside world into most 

American homes. Entertainments have changed, and the constantly-developing motion picture is teaching us about distant regions.

All these changes have caused us to depend upon one another. Farmers need the clothing and machinery which is manufactured in the cities. City workers need the food which is raised on the farms. Southerners need the food products raised in the Middle West, and the Middle West

National Broadcasting Company MASTER CONTROL BOARD IN RADIO CITY. (See also pages 28 and 69.) 

needs the cotton of the South. The Far West needs the manufactured goods of New England, and New England needs the wool from the Mountain States. Hence, the MA- 

CHINE AGE in which we live, along with our rapid systems of communication and transportation, our increased knowledge of the outside world, and our high standards of living have made our life complex-far more complex than that of our ancestors a half or even a quarter of a century ago. 

And the complexity of present-day life has made us dependent on one another as individuals, as communities, as states, and as nations. 

Changing Methods of Earning a Living. The Rise of 

Factories. The MACHINE AGE has brought about another important change a change in the methods of earning a living. The pioneers who lived on the frontier as our nation developed were, in a general way, economically equal.¹ 'That is, equal in the amount of property possessed.

Each family earned its own living, usually by farmingtilling the soil. The necessities of life were theirs for the getting. The soil, the forests, and the streams furnished all their needs. The colonial period was a period of homemade products and the raw materials were practically free and unlimited. It was a period of economic equality.¹ 

But as simple machines were invented, many articles of necessity ceased to be manufactured in the home. Cloth, furniture, and shoes, along with many other commodities, began to be made in factories. The reason for this change was that the machines were expensive every one could not afford to have them. Hence, machines, and factories, began to operate in central locations and to make an article on a large scale; and the owner would sell it to those who could buy it. It was in this simple way that the home-manufacture of common household articles changed to factorymanufacture of them. As time went on, and more machines were invented, more things began to be made in factories. 

Soon villages and cities began to grow up around the factorysites of New England. Some farmers left their self-supporting farms and moved into the factory-towns to work for a wage in the factories. Immigrants who came from foreign lands settled in these factory-towns and began working for wages. (See further discussion on the GROWTH OF CITIES 

in PART THREE, UNIT I, page 113 ff.)

As the nation grew down through the years, more machines were invented, more factories began to operate and produce manufactured articles on a large scale,¹ and more workmen began to work for wages. The growth of factories required business organizations to run them; hence, not only factory workers were needed in the growing cities, ¹Producing a manufactured article on a large scale is commonly called mass production. Raw materials for such production are bought in huge quantities—hence, cheaply. In mass production, labor often becomes very much specialized, one worker performing one particular task all day long, and never doing all the work on a single product, such as a shoe, a chair, or an automobile. How different this method is from production in a pioneer home where the frontiersman built his complete house, made all the furniture it contained, and possibly his wife made all the clothes the family wore!

but also countless numbers of workers in other lines to keep the organizations going. Today, nearly three-quarters of our entire population live in large villages and cities. 

This vast number of people do not earn their living by tilling the soil, as our colonial ancestors did. They earn their living by practicing the professions, carrying on businesses 

Carnegie-Illinois Steel Corp. 

INTERIOR OF A STEEL PLANT IN PENNSYLVANIA 

Tapping a furnace. (See also pictures on pages 315, 332.) of their own (many of which are a result of some invention) and by working for wages. The vast population of our 

Nation today has often been referred to as a wage-earning population. There is much truth to such a statement. 

Changing Economic Conditions; the Rise of Capitalism and Big Businesses. The early New England factories were usually begun by a man or group of men who had some money, or means of getting it, with which to start the fac-

tories. Money was needed to erect the plants, buy the machinery and the raw materials, hire the workers and get the manufactured products on the market. Small concerns could get started with small amounts of money; larger concerns needed more money. The aim, of course, of all concerns was to sell the manufactured product for a price 

A BOARD OF DIRECTOR'S MEETING 

which would bring a profit to the owners. The profits were the owners' pay (often called dividends) for carrying on the business. Often the profits of a good business were used to enlarge the business so more profits would be made in the future.

As factories and business concerns developed, they soon realized that one of their big expenses was the wages paid to their workers (their laborers). Hence, they sought to hire their laborers as cheaply as they could and require

them to do as much work within a day as possible. In the eighties and nineties of the last century, laborers began to organize into unions so that they might more effectively demand higher pay, shorter hours, and better working conditions. And ever since that time, there have gone on in our history struggles between the owners of some large business concerns and the workers-between the so-called capitalists and laborers-in short, between capital and labor.

Not all the big business concerns of today are a direct result of inventions, but most of them, either directly or indirectly, can be traced back to some invention or discovery. Inventions, discoveries, and improved methods of doing things have all played their part in the development of such big business as oil companies; mines; railroad companies; farm-implement companies; steamship companies; motion picture industries; banks; department stores; the steel industries; food-packing plants; and automobile and airplane companies.

In order to get control of large amounts of money (capital) and to expand their industries, big business concerns (usually corporations) have merged themselves into trusts. 

Such mergers have often put millions of dollars under the control of a few men. In order to raise large amounts of money to establish, expand, and develop their concerns, many large business organizations have sold bonds and stocks to the general public. These securities have been used for SPECULATION, because their value rises and falls with the prosperity of the business. Our governments, both state and Federal, have tried to control big business organizations so as to protect the welfare of all the people. Many of the controlling laws have not been entirely successful. 

During the last half or three-quarters of the century, there has developed, then, in our nation, a system which we call capitalism, where a few men have gotten control of large business organizations backed, oftentimes, by millions of dollars of capital. In their effort to make their busi-

nesses as profitable as possible, these huge organizations have sometimes hired their workers as cheaply as they could. This condition led to the formation of workers organizations (labor unions) which have attempted, by various means, to better their conditions. The last half-century of our history has seen frequent struggles between these two groups: On the one side, the controllers and owners of large business and industrial concerns, the capitalists; on the other side, the workers, laborers. 

During the last few years our nation has been experiencing a condition which seems unnecessary in a land of plenty. For various and many complicated reasons, large business concerns have had to reduce production drastically, NO

"No HELP WANTED" 

Millions saw this sign, as the depression spread over the Nation. some to close down almost entirely. Modern machines had replaced many workmen; hence, laborers were without work. When great numbers of wage-earning people ceased to earn, they could not buy the products of industry. This caused a further shut-down of industry, and more wageearners were thrown out of employment. Early in 1933, it was estimated that around 12,000,000 wageearners in the United 

States were out of employment. Savings of many dwindled rapidly, and thousands of people, especially in the large cities, have been kept from starvation by private and public charity. Charitable organiza-

tions and local governments have found it difficult to feed, clothe, and give shelter to the armies of unemployed, because their own revenues, too, have been hard to obtain. 

The spring of 1933 found the United States in one of the most chaotic conditions it has ever been. A depression was upon us.

UNIT II. OUR GOVERNMENTS MUST MEET CHANGING CONDITIONS THE PROBLEM 

When the economic and social conditions of any people change, the government of those people must meet the new conditions. The framers of our Constitution recognized this fact when they made provision in the Constitution for amending it-amending it to meet new conditions. Gov ernmental functions change. GEORGE WASHINGTON never heard of an income tax. It was unnecessary in ANDREW 

JACKSON'S time to enact anti-trust laws. There were no compulsory school attendance laws when ABRAHAM LIN- COLN was a boy. Traffic laws didn't worry THEODORE ROOSE- VELT'S contemporaries. 

This UNIT will help you answer the question: How has our government attempted to meet some of our present day SOCIAL and ECONOMIC CHANGES?

1. CHANGING GOVERNMENT: “THE NEW DEAL” The Origin of the New Deal: March 4, 1933. Conditions grew gradually worse during the fall of 1932. The Presidential and Congressional elections of November were sweeping victories for the Democratic party. Not only was its Presidential candidate elected by an overwhelming majority, but enough Democratic Representatives and Senators were elected to give the party a substantial majority in both houses of Congress. Conditions grew no better between the time of the elections and the inauguration four months later. Among other promises in its campaign, the 

Democratic party had pledged itself if successful in the election, to do all in its power to put the great armies of unemployed men and women back to work and to prevent a recurrence of such bad conditions in the future. Such, then, was the task of PRESIDENT ROOSEVELT, his Cabinet, and the Congress when they took control of the Federal government on March 4, 1933.

PRESIDENT ROOSEVELT described the task in his inaugural address as follows: 

This great nation will endure as it has endured, will revive and will prosper. So first of all let me assert my firm belief that the only thing we have to fear is fear itself—nameless, unreasoning, unjustified terror which paralyzes needed efforts to convert retreat into advance. "In every dark hour of our national life a leadership of frankness and of vigor has met with that understanding and support of the people themselves which is essential to victory and I am convinced that you will again give that support to leadership in these critical days. ... "In such a spirit on my part and on yours we face our common difficulties. . . . Our distress comes from no failure of substance. . . . Compared with the perils which our forefathers conquered because they believed and were not afraid, we have still much to be thankful for. Nature still offers her bounty and human efforts have multiplied it. Plenty is at our doorstep, but a generous use of it languishes in the very sight of the supply. 66 These dark days will be worth all they cost us if they

teach us that our true destiny is not to be ministered unto but to minister to ourselves and to our fellow-men. 。。 “... Our greatest primary task is to put people to work. 

It can be accomplished in part by direct recruiting by the Government itself, treating the task as we would treat the emergency of a war. “... We must act and we must act quickly."

To have the Federal government step in and take control of the industrial and economic order of things was new in the history of our nation; it was the origin of "."

Almost from the very beginning the people of the Nation began to line up either FOR or AGAINST the New Deal policies. Some thought that the Federal government, by taking hold of the industrial and economic order of things, could put men to work again and bring about better times. Others believed that the government could not and should not interfere with the industries of the nation. 

What the "New Deal" Has Attempted to Do and How It

Has Attempted to Do It. Immediately after PRESIDENT 

ROOSEVELT was inaugurated he began to put his ideas into action. He called many men and women to Washington to advise him on the various agencies, BOARDS, COMMISSIONS, and BUREAUS, that he wanted to set up to carry out his New Deal policies. Because so many of these advisers were college and university professors and experts in various lines of work, they began to be called the "Brain Trust." "Brain Trusters" became a common phrase throughout the country.

Congress was quickly convened into special session and passed many acts which gave the President authority to set up the governmental machinery necessary to carry out the New Deal policies. (We shall see later how some of these acts were declared unconstitutional by the Supreme Court.) 

In just a few months' time, a score or more of agencies were set up and put into operation. Soon these agencies of the New Deal began to be referred to by the first letters of the words which named them. Some of the most out-

standing of these "alphabetical agencies” are listed below, after which a few are briefly described: 

A.A.A.-AGRICULTURAL ADJUSTMENT ADMINISTRATION; 

C.A.B.-CONSUMERS' ADVISORY BOARD; C.C.C.-COMMODITY CREDIT CORPORATION; C.W.A.-CIVIL WORKS ADMINISTRA- TION; E.C.W.-EMERGENCY CONSERVATION WORK (official name for CIVILIAN CONSERVATION CORPS); E.H.C.-EMER- GENCY HOUSING CORPORATION; F.A.C.A.-FEDERAL ALCOHOL CONTROL ADMINISTRATION; F.C.A.-FARM CREDIT ADMINIS- TRATION; F.C.T.-FEDERAL CO-ORDINATION OF TRANSPORTA- TION; F.D.I.C.-FEDERAL DEPOSIT INSURANCE CORPORATION; 

F.E.R.A.-FEDERAL EMERGENCY RELIEF ADMINISTRATION; 

F.F.M.C.-FEDERAL FARM MORTGAGE CORPORATION; F.S.H.C.

H.O.L.C.-HOME OWNERS LOAN CORPORATION; I.A.B.— INDUSTRIAL ADVISORY BOARD; N.E.C.-NATIONAL EMER- GENCY COUNCIL; N.L.B.-NATIONAL LABOR BOARD; N.P.B. 

-NATIONAL PLANNING BOARD; N.R.A.-NATIONAL RECOV- ERY ADMINISTRATION; P.A.B.-PETROLEUM ADMINISTRA- TION BOARD; P.I.A.-PETROLEUM INDUSTRY ADMINISTRA- TION; P.W.A.-PUBLIC WORKS ADMINISTRATION; S.A.B.- SCIENCE ADVISORY BOARD; S.E.S.-SOIL EROSION SERVICE; 

T.V.A.-TENNESSEE VALLEY AUTHORITY. 

The National Industrial Recovery Act. Probably the best known of all the New Deal acts was the National Industrial Recovery Act (N.I.R.A.) which was passed by Congress in 

June, 1933, and provided for the National Recovery Administration (N.R.A.). By this Act the Federal government hoped to bring about better conditions by forcing the industries of the country to live up to certain rules. At first a "blanket code" was set up which asked all those who had joined the N.R.A. to pay workmen no less than $12.00 a week; to cut working hours to at least 40 per week; and to hire no child under 14 years of age. The "blanket code" was a temporary measure, and soon "special codes" were devised for the various industries. It was hoped that if employers would live up to the codes that higher wages

would be paid, and that if working hours were limited and child labor abolished, more men would be put to work. If men could be given work at fair wages, conditions would soon improve. So many objections were raised, however, to the efforts of the Federal government to regulate private industries that a TEST CASE was taken to the Supreme Court of the United States. In June of 1935, the Supreme Court 

GOVERNMENT AGENTS DISTRIBUTING COTTON BENEFIT CHECKS IN TENNESSEE 

decided against the N.R.A. by declaring it unconstitutional. 

It said Congress had no right to authorize the President and his aides to regulate private industries. This decision, of course, practically broke up the entire N.R.A. organization. 

The Agricultural Adjustment Act. Economic depressions have always brought bad conditions to the farmers, and the recent one was no exception. Prices of farm produce went so low that in many cases crops were left to rot in the fields, because it did not pay to harvest them. Because

of low incomes, farmers throughout the Nation failed to keep their farms in repair and to meet their obligations. 

The Federal government attempted to bring relief to the farmers by passing the Agricultural Adjustment Act in May, 1933, which provided for the creation of the Agricul tural Adjustment Administration (A.A.A.). The various provisions of the A.A.A. attempted to cut down the production of farm produce such as cotton, rice, tobacco, hogs, and wheat in hopes that prices would rise if less were produced. The farmers were paid in cash by the government for cutting down their acreage and their number of hogs. 

The government raised some of the money with which to pay the farmers by a processing tax. This was a tax on the manufacturing processes which some agricultural products have to go through before being put on the market, such as the grinding of wheat into flour, and the spinning of cotton into cloth.

Other Acts passed to help the farmers were: The Potato 

Control Act; the Farm Credit Act which created the Federal 

Farm Credit Administration (F.F.C.A.) to loan money to farmers at very reasonable interest rates; and the Frazier- 

Lemke Bankruptcy and Farm Mortgage Acts. The A.A.A. 

like the N.R.A.

was declared unconstitutional by the United 

States Supreme Court in January of 1936. 

The Civilian Conservation Corps. The DEPRESSION not only caused millions of mature men to lose their jobs, but it prevented hundreds of thousands of young men from securing gainful employment. The years from 1930 to the spring of 1933 saw an increasing number of older boys and young men wandering about, living by transient charity or becoming an increased burden on their families. 

To relieve this distressing effect of the depression, Congress passed in the Spring of 1933 the Civilian Conservation Corps Reforestation Relief Act which has created the Civil ian Conservation Corps (C.C.C.). The Act gave the President authority to establish camps for boys, most of which have been located in National and State Forest Reserves. Each

camp is under the control of an Army, Navy, or Reserve Corps officer, but the C.C.C. camps do not have strict military or naval discipline. Young men between the ages of 

18 and 25 years, whose families are on relief, may enroll.

There are also a few camps for unemployed World War veterans known as "senior camps." First enrollment in the camps is for a period of six months, but men may re-enroll. 

Men are permitted to leave the camp before their period is up, if they are able to find gainful employment elsewhere. 

The men are required to work at such projects as improving National and State Parks, reforestation, flood and fire prevention, and soil erosion prevention. This work is supervised by the Federal Departments of Agriculture and 

Interior, especially the FORESTRY DIVISION of the DEPART- 

MENT OF AGRICULTURE. In return for their work, the men are provided with their expenses in the camps and are paid at the rate of $30.00 per month, $25.00 of which must be sent home to their distressed families.

The original plans were to enroll about 300,000 young men in C.C.C. camps. However, the scheme seemed so successful to Congress that in the spring of 1935 it passed a bill providing for the expansion of the camps to care for 

600,000 men at one time.

P.W.A., C.W.A., F.E.R.A., and W.W.A. "Work in place of dole" became a slogan of the New Deal. PRESIDENT 

ROOSEVELT and his advisers felt that there were many public projects that could be undertaken by the various governmental units so that the millions of unemployed could be given productive work. By undertaking these public projects, the government tried to accomplish a dual purpose: First, it would create honest work, so men could earn their livelihood instead of being forced to accept public or private charity. Second, it would be making definite public improvements. It was with these aims in mind that Congress and the President set up these various "work-giving" agencies: 

The Public Works Administration (P.W.A.) was an

U. S. Bureau of Reclamation A SENTINEL IN SOUTHEASTERN ARIZONA 

Lands like these are reclaimed by irrigation from the 

Colorado River.

agency created to give employment to great numbers of people in the building of useful public works such as post office buildings, highways, and army and navy posts. 

The Civil Works Administration (C.W.A.) was an agency whereby the Federal government helped state and local governments to make local public improvements by putting their unemployed to work. The Federal government paid 30% of the cost of the projects outright and loaned the remainder to the local government at a very low rate of interest.

The Federal Emergency Relief Administration (F.E.R.A.) 

created by Congress in May, 1933, gave Federal aid to the various states to help them bear their relief burden. 

Through their governors, the states petitioned the F.E.R.A. for funds to carry out relief projects. If the F.E.R.A. found the projects to be worthy and the relief necessary, it granted financial aid to help carry them out. 

In April, 1935, Congress passed the Work Relief Act which created the Works Progress Administration (W.P.A.). The W.P.A. has attempted to pick from the millions of unemployed, those who have been on the relief rolls for a long period of time and give them employment on some public project.

The Tennessee Valley Authority. For years engineers have seen the possibilities of building great power dams on the Tennessee river at Muscle Shoals, Alabama. One such dam was begun during the World War with the idea of generating power for the manufacture of nitrates. The 

War ended, however, before the project was completed, so little national interest was taken in the possibilities of Muscle Shoals until the New Deal again took up the idea. 

In the Spring of 1933, Congress passed the Tennessee Valley Authority Act which gave to a BOARD of three men the Tennessee Valley Authority (T.V.A.). This BOARD has built up a great agency which hopes to construct great power plants for generating electricity, to develop the surrounding farm lands, to plan and build communities, to

plant forests, manufacture nitrates, and generally to improve the Muscle Shoals region. Thousands of workmen will be needed over a long period of time to complete such a program. This, of course, will carry out the New Deal's policy of putting men to work. The United States Supreme 

Court has recently (1936) upheld the right of the Federal government to carry on with the T.V.A.

The National Housing Act. The National Housing Act of 

1933 provided for the creation of the Federal Housing Administration (F.H.A.) to study the problem of building and repairing homes in the United States. It also provided for the Home Owners Loan Corporation (H.O.L.C.) which loaned money to qualified persons to build or remodel homes. The powers of the H.O.L.C. were increased, and the time extended for it to make loans, by the Home Mortgage 

Relief Act of 1935. 

The Social Security Act. The Social Security Act of 1935 

is considered by many as one of the most outstanding Acts of New Deal legislation. By this Act, Congress and the 

President hope to establish certain insurances and pensions which will prevent poverty in old age. The Act provides that the Federal government match any sum of not more than fifteen dollars which any state contributes to the support of a needy person over sixty-five years of age. It also provides for dependent children and mothers, and for oldage and unemployment insurance.

The 1936 Election. During the four years of PRESI- DENT ROOSEVELT's first administration, especially during the later part of the term, there was a great deal of criticism of many of the NEW DEAL policies. The spending of such large sums of money by the Federal government, the kinds of projects that were begun to give men work, the way in which these projects were managed, the many governmental bureaus and departments that were created to carry out the work, the kinds of taxes that were levied to raise money, and the authority the President exercised in carrying out his policies were especially criticized.

"America! America! God shed 

His grace on thee, And crown thy good with brotherhood 

From sea to shining sea."

In November of 1936, at the regular Presidential and Congressional election, the voters of the United States had an opportunity to express their approval or disapproval of the New Deal policies. The Democratic Party had again nominated PRESIDENT ROOSEVELT. His closest opponent was GOVERNOR ALFRED M. LANDON of KANSAS who had been nominated by the Republican Party. When the votes were counted, the results showed one of the greatest Presidential election landslides in the history of the Nation. PRESIDENT 

ROOSEVELT and his VICE-PRESIDENTIAL running mate, JOHN 

N. GARNER, were overwhelmingly re-elected. The Electoral vote was 523 to 8. At the same time the voters elected or re-elected a substantial majority of Democrats to both houses of Congress. 

Paying for the New Deal. The thinking person cannot help asking such questions as: How much has the New 

Deal cost? Where have all the billions of dollars come from to finance such agencies as the T.V.A., C.C.C., W.P.A., and the H.O.L.C.? When and how will this money be paid back?

It has cost billions of dollars. Some of the money has been obtained by taxation such as the processing tax which helped pay for the A.A.A. Some has been raised by the sale of bonds to the people. These bonds, along with the interest on them, will eventually have to be paid back. No doubt great sums of money will have to be raised in the future by some kind of taxation. Paying for all these activities and at the same time keeping the credit of the United States Government good, will be one of the pressing problems that our present and future statesmen will be called upon to solve.

the 1932 election. How many did Mr. Hoover receive? 

Compare these figures with those of the 1928 Presidential election. 

3. FLOOR TALK: Find a copy of President F. D. Roosevelt's first inaugural address. Read it and give your class a floor talk about it. 

4. CLASS REPORT: Try to find in your histories instances when our Federal government has, in the past, attempted to regulate the economic life of the Nation. 

5. FLOOR TALK: Each member of the class take some one New 

Deal agency and, after finding out all you can about it, give a floor talk on it. 

6. DISCUSS: Why have some of the New Deal agencies been declared unconstitutional? 

7. DISCUSS: Should the Supreme Court have the power to overrule Congress and the President? 

8. DESCRIBE SOME PROJECT to your class that you know about that was done under the direction of some New Deal agency such as the C.C.C., C.W.A., P.W.A., or W.P.A. 

9. IF POSSIBLE, bring in to the class and discuss a United States 

Government bond. 

10. DISCUSS what is meant by this statement: "Other generations will still be paying for the New Deal.”

APPENDIX .CONSTITUTION OF THE UNITED STATES OF AMERICA PREAMBLE 

We the people of the United States, in order to form a more perfect union, establish justice, insure domestic tranquillity, provide for the common defence, promote the general welfare, and secure the blessings of liberty to ourselves and our posterity, do ordain and establish this CONSTITUTION for the United States of America. 

ARTICLE I.-LEGISLATIVE DEPARTMENT SECTION 1.- CONGRESS 

All legislative powers herein granted shall be vested in a Congress of the United States, which shall consist of a Senate and a House of Representatives. 

SECTION 2.- HOUSE OF REPRESENTATIVES CLAUSE (1) The House of Representatives shall be composed of members chosen every second year by the people of the several States, and the electors in each State shall have the qualifications requisite for electors of the most numerous branch of the State Legislature. 

CLAUSE (2) No person shall be a Representative who shall not have attained to the age of twenty-five years, and been seven years a citizen of the United States, and who shall not, when elected, be an inhabitant of that State in which he shall be chosen. 

1 CLAUSE (3) Representatives and direct taxes shall be apportioned among the several States which may be included within this Union, according to their respective numbers, which shall be determined by adding to the whole number of free persons, including those bound to service for a term of years, and excluding Indians not taxed, three-fifths of all other persons. The actual enumeration shall be made within three years after the first meeting of the Congress of the United States, and within every subsequent term of ten years, in such manner as they shall by law direct. The number of Representatives shall not exceed one for every thirty thousand, but each State shall have at least one representative; and until such enumeration shall be made, the State of New Hampshire shall be entitled to choose three, Massachusetts eight, Rhode Island and Providence 

Plantations one, Connecticut five, New York six, New Jersey four, Pennsylvania eight, Delaware one, Maryland six, Virginia ten, North 

Carolina five, South Carolina five, and Georgia three. 

'Changed by Amendment XIV, Section 2, and Amendment XVI. 

395

CLAUSE (4) When vacancies happen in the representation from any State, the Executive authority thereof shall issue writs of election to fill such vacancies. 

CLAUSE (5) The House of Representatives shall choose their 

Speaker and other officers; and shall have the sole power of impeachment.

SECTION 3.- SENATE 1 CLAUSE (1) [The Senate of the United States shall be composed of two Senators from each State, chosen by the Legislature thereof, for six years; and each Senator shall have one vote.]

CLAUSE (2) Immediately after they shall be assembled in consequence of the first election, they shall be divided as equally as may be into three classes. The seats of the Senators of the first class shall be vacated at the expiration of the second year, of the second class at the expiration of the fourth year, and of the third class at the expiration of the sixth year, so that one-third may be chosen every second year; ¹[and if vacancies happen by resignation, or otherwise, during the recess of the Legislature of any State, the Executive thereof may make temporary appointments until the next meeting of the Legislature, which shall then fill such vacancies]. 

CLAUSE (3) No person shall be a Senator who shall not have at tained to the age of thirty years, and been nine years a citizen of the United States, and who shall not, when elected, be an inhabitant of that State for which he shall be chosen. 

CLAUSE (4) The Vice-President of the United States shall be 

President of the Senate, but shall have no vote, unless they be equally divided.

CLAUSE (5) The Senate shall choose their other officers, and also a President pro tempore, in the absence of the Vice-President, or when he shall exercise the office of President of the United States. 

CLAUSE (6) The Senate shall have the sole power to try all impeachments. When sitting for that purpose, they shall be on oath or affirmation. When the President of the United States is tried, the 

Chief Justice shall preside: and no person shall be convicted without the concurrence of two-thirds of the members present. 

CLAUSE (7) Judgment in cases of impeachment shall not extend further than to removal from office, and disqualification to hold and enjoy any office of honor, trust, or profit under the United States: but the party convicted shall nevertheless be liable and subject to indictment, trial, judgment, and punishment, according to law. 

SECTION 4.- ELECTIONS AND SESSIONS (BOTH HOUSES) 

CLAUSE (1) The times, places, and manner of holding elections for Senators and Representatives shall be prescribed in each State by the Legislature thereof; but the Congress may at any time by law make or alter such regulations, except as to the places of choosing Senators. 

1 Clause 1 and part of Clause 2, in Section 3, have been changed by Amendment XVII.

Federal Constitution 

397 

1 CLAUSE (2) The Congress shall assemble at least once in every year, and such meeting shall be on the first Monday in December, unless they shall by law appoint a different day. 

SECTION 5.- RULES OF PROCEDURE CLAUSE (1) Each House shall be the judge of the elections, re turns, and qualifications of its own members, and a majority of each shall constitute a quorum to do business; but a smaller number may adjourn from day to day, and may be authorized to compel the attendance of absent members, in such manner, and under such penalties, as each House may provide.

CLAUSE (2) Each House may determine the rules of its proceedings, punish its members for disorderly behavior, and, with the concurrence of two-thirds, expel a member.

CLAUSE (3) Each House shall keep a journal of its proceedings, and from time to time publish the same, excepting such parts as may in their judgment require secrecy; and the yeas and nays of the members of either House on any question shall, at the desire of onefifth of those present, be entered on the journal.

CLAUSE (4) Neither House, during the session of Congress, shall, without the consent of the other, adjourn for more than three days, nor to any other place than that in which the two Houses shall be sitting.

SECTION 6.- SALARY, PRIVILEGES, AND PROHIBITIONS OF MEMBERS CLAUSE (1) The Senators and Representatives shall receive a compensation for their services, to be ascertained by law, and paid out of the treasury of the United States. They shall in all cases, except treason, felony, and breach of the peace, be privileged from arrest during their attendance at the session of their respective Houses, and in going to and returning from the same; and for any speech or debate in either House they shall not be questioned in any other place.

CLAUSE (2) No Senator or Representative shall, during the time for which he was elected, be appointed to any civil office under the authority of the United States, which shall have been created, or the emoluments whereof shall have been increased, during such time; and no person holding any office under the United States shall be a member of either House during his continuance in office. 

SECTION 7.- METHOD OF MAKING LAWS CLAUSE (1) All bills for raising revenue shall originate in the 

House of Representatives; but the Senate may propose or concur with amendments as on other bills.

CLAUSE (2) Every bill which shall have passed the House of Representatives and the Senate shall, before it become a law, be presented to the President of the United States; if he approve he shall sign it, but if not he shall return it with his objections to that House in which it shall have originated, who shall enter the objections at 

1 Changed by Amendment XX, Section 2.

large on their journal, and proceed to reconsider it. If after such reconsideration two-thirds of that House shall agree to pass the bill, it shall be sent, together with the objections, to the other House, by which it shall likewise be reconsidered, and, if approved by twothirds of that House, it shall become a law. But in all such cases the votes of both Houses shall be determined by yeas and nays, and the names of the persons voting for and against the bill shall be entered on the journal of each House respectively. If any bill shall not be returned by the President within ten days (Sundays excepted) after it shall have been presented to him, the same shall be a law, in like manner as if he had signed it, unless the Congress by their adjournment prevent its return, in which case it shall not be a law. 

CLAUSE (3) Every order, resolution, or vote to which the concurrence of the Senate and House of Representatives may be necessary (except on a question of adjournment) shall be presented to the President of the United States; and, before the same shall take effect, shall be approved by him, or, being disapproved by him, shall be repassed by two-thirds of the Senate and House of Representatives, according to the rules and limitations prescribed in the case of a bill.

SECTION 8.- POWERS OF CONGRESS 

The Congress shall have power,— 

CLAUSE (1) To lay and collect taxes, duties, imposts, and excises, to pay the debts and provide for the common defence and general welfare of the United States; but all duties, imposts, and excises shall be uniform throughout the United States; 

CLAUSE (2) To borrow money on the credit of the United States;

CLAUSE (3) To regulate commerce with foreign nations, and among the several States, and with the Indian tribes; 

CLAUSE (4) To establish a uniform rule of naturalization, and uniform laws on the subject of bankruptcies throughout the United States; 

CLAUSE (5) To coin money, regulate the value thereof, and of foreign coin, and fix the standard of weights and measures; 

CLAUSE (6) To provide for the punishment of counterfeiting the securities and current coin of the United States;

CLAUSE (7) To establish postoffices and postroads; 

CLAUSE (8) To promote the progress of science and useful arts, by securing for limited times to authors and inventors the exclusive right to their respective writings and discoveries; 

CLAUSE (9) To constitute tribunals inferior to the Supreme Court; 

CLAUSE (10) To define and punish piracies and felonies commit ted on the high seas, and offences against the law of nations; 

CLAUSE (11) To declare war, grant letters of marque and reprisal, and make rules concerning captures on land and water; 

CLAUSE (12) To raise and support armies, but no appropriation of money to that use shall be for a longer term than two years; 

CLAUSE (13) To provide and maintain a navy;

Federal Constitution 

399 To make rules for the government and regulation naval forces;

CLAUSE (14) 

of the land and

CLAUSE (15) To provide for calling forth the militia to execute the laws of the Union, suppress insurrections, and repel invasions; 

CLAUSE (16) To provide for organizing, arming, and disciplining the militia, and for governing such part of them as may be employed in the service of the United States, reserving to the States respectively, the appointment of the officers, and the authority of training the militia according to the discipline prescribed by Congress; 

CLAUSE (17) To exercise exclusive legislation, in all cases whatsoever, over such district (not exceeding ten miles square) as may, by cession of particular States, and the acceptance of Congress, become the seat of the government of the United States; and to exercise like authority over all places purchased by the consent of the Legislature of the State in which the same shall be, for the erection of forts, magazines, arsenals, dock-yards, and other needful buildings;- and 

1 CLAUSE (18) To make all laws which shall be necessary and proper for carrying into execution the foregoing powers, and all other powers vested by this Constitution in the government of the United States, or in any department or officer thereof. 

SECTION 9.- POWERS DENIED TO CONGRESS 

CLAUSE (1) The migration or importation of such persons as any of the States now existing shall think proper to admit, shall not be prohibited by the Congress prior to the year one thousand eight hundred and eight, but a tax or duty may be imposed on such importation, not exceeding ten dollars for each person. 

CLAUSE (2) The privilege of the writ of habeas corpus shall not be suspended, unless when in cases of rebellion or invasion the public safety may require it.

CLAUSE (3) No bill of attainder or ex post facto law shall be passed. 

2 CLAUSE (4) No capitation or other direct tax shall be laid, unless in proportion to the census or enumeration herein before directed to be taken.

CLAUSE (5) No tax or duty shall be laid on articles exported from any State.

CLAUSE (6) No preference shall be given by any regulation of commerce or revenue to the ports of one State over those of another; nor shall vessels bound to, or from, one State, be obliged to enter, clear, or pay duties in another.

CLAUSE (7) No money shall be drawn from the treasury, but in consequence of appropriations made by law; and a regular statement and account of the receipts and expenditures of all public money shall be published from time to time. 

1The famous "Elastic Clause" or "Implied Powers" Clause. 

2 Changed by Amendment XVI.

CLAUSE (8) No title of nobility shall be granted by the United 

States; and no person holding any office of profit or trust under them shall, without the consent of the Congress, accept of any present, emolument, office, or title, of any kind whatever, from any king, prince, or foreign state.

SECTION 10.- POWERS DENIED TO THE STATES 

CLAUSE (1) No State shall enter into any treaty, alliance, or confederation; grant letters of marque and reprisal; coin money; emit bills of credit; make anything but gold and silver coin a tender in payment of debts; pass any bill of attainder, ex post facto law, or law impairing the obligation of contracts, or grant any title of nobility.

CLAUSE (2) No State shall, without the consent of the Congress, lay any imposts or duties on imports or exports, except what may be absolutely necessary for executing its inspection laws; and the net produce of all duties and imposts, laid by any State on imports or exports, shall be for the use of the treasury of the United States; and all such laws shall be subject to the revision and control of the Congress.

CLAUSE (3) No State shall, without the consent of Congress, lay any duty of tonnage, keep troops or ships of war in time of peace, enter into any agreement or compact with another State, or with a foreign power, or engage in war, unless actually invaded, or in such imminent danger as will not admit of delay.

ARTICLE II.— EXECUTIVE DEPARTMENT SECTION 1.- THE PRESIDENT AND VICE-PRESIDENT CLAUSE (1) The executive power shall be vested in a President of the United States of America. He shall hold his office during the term of four years, and, together with the Vice-President, chosen for the same term, be elected as follows:- 

CLAUSE (2) Each State shall appoint, in such manner as the Legislature thereof may direct, a number of Electors equal to the whole number of Senators and Representatives to which the State may be entitled in the Congress: but no Senator or Representative, or person holding an office of trust or profit under the United States, shall be appointed an Elector. 

1 CLAUSE (3) [The Electors shall meet in their respective States, and vote by ballot for two persons, of whom one at least shall not be an inhabitant of the same State with themselves. And they shall make a list of all the persons voted for, and of the number of votes for each; which list they shall sign and certify, and transmit sealed to the seat of the government of the United States, directed to the President of the Senate. The President of the Senate shall, in the presence of the Senate and House of Representatives, open all the 

1 Changed by Amendment XII.

Federal Constitution 

401 certificates, and the votes shall then be counted. The person having the greatest number of votes shall be the President, if such number be a majority of the whole number of Electors appointed; and if there be more than one who have such majority, and have an equal number of votes, then the House of Representatives shall immediately choose by ballot one of them for President; and if no person have a majority, then from the five highest on the list the said House shall in like manner choose the President. But in choosing the President, the votes shall be taken by States, the representation from each State having one vote; a quorum for this purpose shall consist of a member or members from two-thirds of the States, and a majority of all the States shall be necessary to a choice. In every case, after the choice of the President, the person having the greatest number of votes of the Electors shall be the Vice-President. But if there should remain two or more who have equal votes, the Senate shall choose from them by ballot the Vice-President.]

CLAUSE (4) Congress may determine the time of choosing the Electors, and the day on which they shall give their votes; which day shall be the same throughout the United States.

CLAUSE (5) No person except a natural-born citizen, or a citizen of the United States at the time of the adoption of this Constitution, shall be eligible to the office of President; neither shall any person be eligible to that office who shall not have attained to the age of thirty-five years, and been fourteen years a resident within the

United States. 

CLAUSE (6) In case of the removal of the President from office, or of his death, resignation, or inability to discharge the powers and duties of the said office, the same shall devolve on the Vice-President, and the Congress may by law provide for the case of removal, death, resignation, or inability, both of the President and Vice-President, declaring what officer shall then act as President, and such officer shall act accordingly, until the disability be removed, or a President shall be elected.

CLAUSE (7) The President shall, at stated times, receive for his services a compensation, which shall neither be increased nor diminished during the period for which he shall have been elected, and he shall not receive within that period any other emolument from the 

United States, or any of them.

CLAUSE (8) Before he enter on the execution of his office, he shall take the following oath or affirmation:-"I do solemnly swear (or affirm) that I will faithfully execute the office of President of the United States, and will, to the best of my ability, preserve, protect, and defend the Constitution of the United States."

SECTION 2.- POWERS OF THE PRESIDENT CLAUSE (1) The President shall be commander-in-chief of the army and navy of the United States, and of the militia of the several

States, when called into the actual service of the United States; he may require the opinion, in writing, of the principal officer in each of the executive departments, upon any subject relating to the duties of their respective offices, and he shall have power to grant reprieves and pardons for offences against the United States, except in cases of impeachment.

CLAUSE (2) He shall have power, by and with the advice and consent of the Senate, to make treaties, provided two-thirds of the Senators present concur; and he shall nominate, and, by and with the advice and consent of the Senate, shall appoint ambassadors, other public ministers, and consuls, judges of the Supreme Court, and all other officers of the United States, whose appointments are not herein otherwise provided for, and which shall be established by law; but the Congress may by law vest the appointment of such inferior officers, as they think proper, in the President alone, in the courts of law, or in the heads of departments. 

CLAUSE (3) The President shall have power to fill up all vacancies that may happen during the recess of the Senate, by granting commissions which shall expire at the end of their next session. 

SECTION 3.- DUTIES OF THE PRESIDENT 

He shall from time to time give to the Congress information of the state of the Union, and recommend to their consideration such measures as he shall judge necessary and expedient; he may, on extraordinary occasions, convene both Houses, or either of them, and in case of disagreement between them, with respect to the time of adjournment, he may adjourn them to such time as he shall think proper; he shall receive ambassadors and other public ministers; he shall take care that the laws be faithfully executed, and shall commission all the officers of the United States. 

SECTION 4.- REMOVAL FROM OFFICE 

The President, Vice-President, and all civil officers of the United States, shall be removed from office on impeachment for, and conviction of, treason, bribery, or other high crimes and misdemeanors.

ARTICLE III.— JUDICIAL DEPARTMENT SECTION 1.- COURTS AND JUDGES 

The judicial power of the United States shall be vested in one Supreme Court, and in such inferior courts as the Congress may from time to time ordain and establish. The judges, both of the 

Supreme and inferior courts, shall hold their offices during good behavior, and shall, at stated times, receive for their services a compensation, which shall not be diminished during their continuance in office.

Federal Constitution 

403 

SECTION 2.- JURISDICTION OF COURTS 1 CLAUSE (1) The judicial power shall extend to all cases, in law and equity, arising under this Constitution, the laws of the United States, and treaties made, or which shall be made, under their authority; to all cases affecting ambassadors, other public ministers and consuls; to all cases of admiralty and maritime jurisdiction; to controversies to which the United States shall be a party; to controversies between two or more States, between a State and citizens of another State, between citizens of different States, between citizens of the same State claiming lands under grants of different States, and between a State, or the citizens thereof, and foreign states, citizens, or subjects.

CLAUSE (2) In all cases affecting ambassadors, other public ministers, and consuls, and those in which a State shall be party, the Supreme Court shall have original jurisdiction. In all the other cases before mentioned, the Supreme Court shall have appellate jurisdiction, both as to law and fact, with such exceptions, and under such regulations, as the Congress shall make.

CLAUSE (3) The trial of all crimes, except in cases of impeachment, shall be by jury; and such trial shall be held in the State where the said crimes shall have been committed; but when not committed within any State, the trial shall be at such place or places as the 

Congress may by law have directed.

SECTION 3.- TREASON CLAUSE (1) Treason against the United States shall consist only in levying war against them, or in adhering to their enemies, giving them aid and comfort. No person shall be convicted of treason unless on the testimony of two witnesses to the same overt act, or on confession in open court.

CLAUSE (2) The Congress shall have power to declare the punish ment of treason, but no attainder of treason shall work corruption of blood, or forfeiture, except during the life of the person attainted. 

ARTICLE IV.- RELATIONS OF THE STATES TO ONE ANOTHER SECTION 1.- OFFICIAL ACTS 

Full faith and credit shall be given in each State to the public acts, records, and judicial proceedings of every other State. And the

Congress may by general laws prescribe the manner in which such acts, records, and proceedings shall be proved, and the effect thereof. 

SECTION 2.- RIGHTS OF AND RESTRICTIONS ON CITIZENS CLAUSE (1) The citizens of each State shall be entitled to all privileges and immunities of citizens in the several States. 

CLAUSE (2) A person charged in any State with treason, felony, or other crime, who shall flee from justice, and be found in another 

1 Changed by Amendment XI.

State, shall, on demand of the executive authority of the State from which he fled, be delivered up, to be removed to the State having jurisdiction of the crime. 

1 CLAUSE (3) No person held to service or labor in one State, under the laws thereof, escaping into another, shall, in consequence of any law or regulation therein, be discharged from such service or labor, but shall be delivered up on claim of the party to whom such service or labor may be due.

SECTION 3.- NEW STATES AND POSSESSIONS 

CLAUSE (1) New States may be admitted by the Congress into this Union; but no new State shall be formed or erected within the jurisdiction of any other State; nor any State be formed by the junction of two or more States, or parts of States, without the consent of the Legislatures of the States concerned, as well as of the Congress. 

CLAUSE (2) The Congress shall have power to dispose of and make all needful rules and regulations respecting the territory or other property belonging to the United States; and nothing in this Constitution shall be so construed as to prejudice any claims of the United States, or of any particular State. 

SECTION 4.- PROTECTION OF STATES 

The United States shall guarantee to every State in this Union a republican form of government, and shall protect each of them against invasion; and on application of the Legislature, or of the 

Executive (when the Legislature can not be convened), against domestic violence.

ARTICLE V.— AMENDMENTS 

The Congress, whenever two-thirds of both houses shall deem it necessary, shall propose amendments to this Constitution, or, on the application of the Legislatures of two-thirds of the several States, shall call a convention for proposing amendments, which, in either case, shall be valid to all intents and purposes, as part of this Constitution, when ratified by the Legislatures of three-fourths of the several States, or by conventions in three-fourths thereof, as the one or the other mode of ratification may be proposed by the Congress; provided that no amendment which may be made prior to the year one thousand eight hundred and eight shall in any manner affect the first and fourth clauses in the ninth section of the first article; and that no State, without its consent, shall be deprived of its equal suffrage in the Senate.

ARTICLE VI.- GENERAL PROVISIONS 

CLAUSE (1) All debts contracted and engagements entered into, before the adoption of this Constitution shall be as valid against the United States under this Constitution as under the Confederation. 

1 Changed by Amendment XIII.

Federal Constitution 

405 CLAUSE (2) This Constitution, and the laws of the United States which shall be made in pursuance thereof, and all treaties made, or which shall be made, under the authority of the United States, shall be the supreme law of the land; and the judges in every State shall be bound thereby, anything in the constitution or laws of any State to the contrary notwithstanding.

CLAUSE (3) The Senators and Representatives before mentioned, and the members of the several State Legislatures, and all executive and judicial officers, both of the United States and of the several States, shall be bound by oath or affirmation to support this Constitution: but no religious test shall ever be required as a qualification to any office or public trust under the United States. 

ARTICLE VII.-RATIFICATION 

The ratification of the conventions of nine States shall be sufficient for the establishment of this Constitution between the States so ratifying the same.

Done in Convention, by the unanimous consent of the States present, the seventeenth day of September, in the year of our Lord one thousand seven hundred and eighty-seven, and of the Independence of the United States of America the twelfth. In Witness whereof we have hereunto subscribed our names.
